An introduction to university physics for Fidello.

In this article, I explain to Fidello from Foci's Discord server some prerequisite knowledge he needs to effectively derive the equations of motion for a double rigid pendulum and then cover more advanced topics for him. In theory, the derivation of the equations of motion for a double rigid pendulum can be done with Newtonian mechanics. Newtonian mechanics is the formulation of classical mechanics that utilizes Newton's laws of motion, most importantly his second law \(\textbf{F}=m\mathbf{a}\). Classical mechanics is, as its name suggests, physics based on old-fashioned (or classical) notions of forces, determinism and absolute time. It does not account for relativistic effects nor quantum effects.

Given our second pendulum is in a moving reference frame and Newtonian mechanics does not easily adapt to alternative coordinate systems, like polar coordinates, Lagrangian mechanics makes the equations of motion easier to derive. Lagrangian mechanics also leads nicely into other fields of physics such as Hamiltonian mechanics, the Hamilton-Jacobi equations, quantum mechanics and relativity.

Figure shows an animation of the double rigid pendulum. This page does interactive simulations of this system if you would like to specify the parameter values yourself. Code Listing is the Julia code used to generate the above double pendulum animation. I will mention that the Julia code included in this article was largely generated with the help of ChatGPT.

Double pendulum simulation code. 

using DifferentialEquations, LaTeXStrings
using Plots
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Parameters
# ------------------------------------------------------------

g   = 9.81
l₁  = 1.0
l₂  = 1.0

m₁ᵣ = 1.0
m₁ᵦ = 1.0
m₂ᵣ = 1.0
m₂ᵦ = 1.0
fps = 30

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Equations of motion
#
# u = [θ₁, θ₂, θ̇₁, θ̇₂]
# ------------------------------------------------------------

function double_pendulum!(du, u, p, t)

    θ₁, θ₂, θ̇₁, θ̇₂ = u

    Δ = θ₁ - θ₂

    # Common terms
    M₁ = m₁ᵣ/12 + m₁ᵦ + m₂ᵦ

    D₂ = (m₂ᵣ/12 + m₂ᵦ) * l₂ -
         (m₂ᵦ^2 * l₂^2 * cos(Δ)^2) / M₁

    F₁ = m₂ᵦ * l₂ * θ̇₂^2 * sin(Δ) +
         g * cos(θ₁) *
         (m₁ᵣ/2 + m₂ᵣ + m₁ᵦ + m₂ᵦ)

    F₂ = m₂ᵦ * (
             l₁ * θ̇₁^2 * sin(Δ) -
             g * cos(θ₂)
         )

    # Equations from the supplied expressions
    du[1] = θ̇₁
    du[2] = θ̇₂

    du[3] = -F₁ / (M₁ * l₁)

    du[4] = (
        (m₂ᵦ * cos(Δ) / M₁) * F₁ +
        F₂
    ) / D₂
end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial conditions
# ------------------------------------------------------------

θ₁₀  = 0.0
θ₂₀  = 0.0
θ̇₁₀ = 0.0
θ̇₂₀ = 0.0

u₀ = [θ₁₀, θ₂₀, θ̇₁₀, θ̇₂₀]

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Solve
# ------------------------------------------------------------

tspan = (0.0, 60.0)

prob = ODEProblem(double_pendulum!, u₀, tspan)

sol = solve(
    prob,
    Tsit5(),
    saveat=1/fps,
    abstol=1e-9,
    reltol=1e-9
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Coordinates of the two bobs
#
# The angles in the supplied equations are measured from the
# horizontal, hence:
#
# x₁ = l₁ cos(θ₁)
# y₁ = l₁ sin(θ₁)
#
# x₂ = x₁ + l₂ cos(θ₂)
# y₂ = y₁ + l₂ sin(θ₂)
# ------------------------------------------------------------

x₁ = l₁ .* cos.(sol[1, :])
y₁ = l₁ .* sin.(sol[1, :])

x₂ = x₁ .+ l₂ .* cos.(sol[2, :])
y₂ = y₁ .+ l₂ .* sin.(sol[2, :])

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Animation
# ------------------------------------------------------------

xmin = minimum(x₂) - 0.2
xmax = maximum(x₂) + 0.2
ymin = minimum(y₂) - 0.2
ymax = maximum(y₂) + 0.2

anim = @animate for i in eachindex(sol.t)

    plot(
        [0, x₁[i], x₂[i]],
        [0, y₁[i], y₂[i]],
        linewidth=3,
        label=false,
        xlims=(xmin, xmax),
        ylims=(ymin, ymax),
        aspect_ratio=:equal,
        xlabel=L"x\ (\mathrm{metres})",
        ylabel=L"y\ (\mathrm{metres})"
    )

    scatter!(
        [x₁[i], x₂[i]],
        [y₁[i], y₂[i]],
        markersize=8,
        label=false
    )
end

gif(anim, "Double_pendulum.gif", fps=fps)

Prerequisite mathematics

There are several pieces of prerequisite mathematics I need to cover in order for this article to make sense for you.

Mathematical symbols

SymbolMeaning
\(\stackrel{\text{def}}{=}\)Defined as.
\(\forall\)For all.
\(\implies\)Implies or follows.
\(\in\)Element of.
\(\mathbb{R}\)Set of all real numbers.
\(\mathbb{Z}\)Set of all integers.
\(\mathbb{Z}^{+}\)Set of all positive integers.
\(\therefore\)Therefore.

Maclaurin and Taylor series

If we have a function \(f(x)\), it is usually possible to write an infinite sum (or series) that equals \(f(x)\) using \(f(x)\)'s derivatives. Namely:

\[\begin{align} f(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=a} \dfrac{(x-a)^n}{n!} \tag{1} \\ &= f(a) + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=a} \dfrac{(x-a)^n}{n!} . \tag{2} \end{align}\]

Where we are using \(\dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=a}\) to denote the \(n\)th derivative of \(f(x)\) evaluated at \(x=a\) and \(n!\) denotes the factorial of \(n\). If you are unfamiliar, well \(n!=n\times (n-1) \times (n-2) \times(n-3) \times ... \times 1\). For instance, \(5!=5\times 4 \times 3 \times 2 \times 1=120\). As for \(0!\) it is equal to 1. As for \(a\) it is an arbitrary number we get to choose. Equation (1) is known as the Taylor series of \(f(x)\). If we truncate our Taylor series at some \(n\) value, we will get an \(n\)th-order approximation for our function that is called a Taylor polynomial. For instance, if the maximum \(n\) value we substitute in is \(5\), we will get a fifth-order approximation to our function. Keep in mind that our \(n\)th-order approximation includes the \(n\)th derivative of our function evaluated at \(x=a\).

If \(a=0\), then we get the Maclaurin series:

\[\begin{align} f(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=0} \dfrac{x^n}{n!} \tag{3} \\ &= f(0) + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=0} \dfrac{x^n}{n!}. \tag{4} \end{align}\]

Why do these equations hold? Well, try taking the \(n\)th derivative of Equations (1) and (3) and substituting \(x=a\) and \(x=0\), respectively. You will find that you get \(\dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=a}\) and \(\dfrac{d^n f}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=0}\), respectively. Consequently, our series matches our original function in its \(n\)th derivative for \(n\in \mathbb{Z}^{+}\). Likewise, if you substitute in \(x=a\) and \(x=0\) into the Taylor and Maclaurin series, respectively, you should get \(f(a)\) and \(f(0)\), respectively. So we see that this is like a polynomial of infinite order that matches our original function for its value and derivatives value at one point. It turns out that this means it contains enough information to match our original function at other points, too.

There are exceptions where Taylor series do not accurately compute the function they pertain to. For instance, points where the function is undefined can mess things up. As an example, \(\ln{(x+1)}\)'s Maclaurin series will only approximate \(f(x)\) well for \(-1 < x \leq 1\) as at \(x=-1\) the function is undefined. Beyond these points, it will fail to converge (meaning it will not equal some finite number as terms for higher \(n\) will not get rapidly smaller as \(n\) gets bigger).

Now, I know this probably seems pretty abstract to you right now. But let us try to figure out the Maclaurin series for some example functions for which the \(n\)th derivative is fairly easy to calculate. Say, for instance, we calculate the Maclaurin series for \(e^x\). Why \(e^x\)? Because the \(n\)th derivative of \(e^x\) is just \(e^x\), and evaluating this at \(x=0\) yields 1. Hence:

\[\begin{align} e^x &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{x^n}{n!} \tag{5} \end{align}\]
is the Maclaurin series of \(e^x\). Returning to our previous discussion on order approximations, the first-order approximation to \(e^x\) based on this Maclaurin series would be the Maclaurin series terms up until \(n=1\). Which for \(e^x\) would be \(1+x\). Likewise, a second-order approximation of \(e^x\) would be \(1+x+\dfrac{x^2}{2}\). The higher order the approximation, the closer it is likely to be to the true value of our function.

Okay, let us try our hand at another function's Maclaurin series. How about \(\cos{x}\)? We know the first derivative of \(\cos{x}\) is \(-\sin{x}\). Hence the second derivative is \(-\cos{x}\). The third derivative would be \(\sin{x}\). Fourth derivative would be \(\cos{x}\). Fifth derivative would be \(-\sin{x}\) and sixth derivative would be \(-\cos{x}\). Well, I have noticed a pattern here! Seems to me like \((-1)^{\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\rfloor}\) (where \(\lfloor ... \rfloor\) rounds down decimals, e.g. it would take 3.6 and return 3) will recreate the sign of this function. If \(n\) is even, the trigonometric function will be \(\cos{x}\) and if it is odd it will be \(\sin{x}\). Evaluating these functions at \(x=0\), we see that if \(n\) is even we will get \(1\) from the trigonometric function and 0 if \(n\) is odd. Hence:

\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \dfrac{d^nf}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=0} &= \begin{cases} (-1)^{\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\rfloor} & n\hspace{0.1cm}\mathrm{even} \\ 0 & n \hspace{0.1cm}\mathrm{odd}. \end{cases} \end{alignedat}\]
So our sum will essentially just be over even numbers. One way we can denote a sum over even numbers is by replacing \(n\) with \(2n\) and summing over all numbers. As \(2\) times any integer is always an even number. In this event, our \((-1)^{\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\rfloor}\) becomes:

\[\begin{align} (-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n+1}{2}\rfloor} &=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n+1}{2}\rfloor} \notag \\ &= (-1)^{\lfloor n+\frac{1}{2}\rfloor} \notag \\ &= (-1)^{n}. \tag{6} \end{align}\]
We arrived at Equation (6) simply because \(n\) is an integer so adding a half to it should make no difference when we are rounding down. Consequently, our Maclaurin series becomes:

\[\begin{aligned} \cos{x} &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n x^{2n}}{(2n)!} \end{aligned}\]

As our powers of \(x\) are all even in this series, a first-order approximation of \(\cos{x}\) based on this Maclaurin series would be just \(1\) (as the next term in the series would be \(-\dfrac{x^2}{2}\) which is second order and hence not included).

So here is some homework for you: try constructing the Maclaurin series for \(\sin{x}\). You should arrive at:

\[\begin{aligned} \sin{x} &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^nx^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}. \end{aligned}\]

As another example, we will find the Maclaurin series for \(\ln{(x+1)}\) so we can see why it only converges well for \(|x| < -1\) where \(|x|\) is the absolute value of \(x\). The first derivative is \(\dfrac{1}{x+1}\). Second derivative is \(-\dfrac{1}{(x+1)^2}\). Third derivative is \(\dfrac{2}{(x+1)^3}\). Fourth derivative is \(-\dfrac{6}{(x+1)^4}\). So it seems to me that the pattern is that the numerator is \((-1)^{n-1} (n-1)!\) and the denominator is \((x+1)^n\). Hence:

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d^n}{dx^n} \ln{(x+1)} &= \dfrac{(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!}{(x+1)^n}, \hspace{0.1cm} n \geq 1. \tag{7} \end{align}\]
And:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d^n}{dx^n} \ln{(x+1)} \Big|_{x=0} &= (-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!, \hspace{0.1cm} n \geq 1. \tag{8} \end{align}\]
Hence our Maclaurin series is:
\[\begin{align} \ln{(x+1)} &= \ln{1} + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)! x^n}{n!} \tag{9} \\ &= \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^{n-1}x^n}{n}. \tag{10} \end{align}\]
If \(|x|>1\), we would expect the power of \(x\) in our numerator to potentially grow faster than \(n\) and hence our terms could become progressively larger as \(n\rightarrow \infty\). As for if \(x=\pm 1\), well our numerator would be smaller than our denominator, but our denominator would grow rather slowly, which means we would have to calculate a lot of terms to figure out what value it ends up equalling, assuming it does converge. If \(x=1\), we get:
\[\begin{align} \ln{2} &= \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}. \tag{11} \end{align}\]
Which is actually true. As this sum does, indeed, equal \(\ln{2}\). But because the denominator grows very slowly, we would need a lot of terms in the series in order to get a good approximation for what it equals. If \(x=-1\), then we get:
\[\begin{align} \ln{0} &= \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^{n-1} (-1)^{n}}{n} \tag{12} \\ &= -\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{1}{n}. \tag{13} \end{align}\]
This sum does not converge, hence it actually equals \(\infty\). Hence why \(|x| < 1\) is a requirement for this Maclaurin series to converge, or at least converge at a reasonable pace, although it does also converge, albeit slowly, for \(x=1\).

Maclaurin and Taylor series have broad applications in physics. One application they have is in helping us connect more general and robust laws of physics to older approximations. For more on this, see the section on the classical limit of special relativity's kinetic energy.

Complex numbers

Complex numbers are numbers that involve both a real and an imaginary part. That is, they are of the form \(a+bi\) where \(a\) and \(b\) are both real numbers and \(i\) is the imaginary number. \(a\) is called the real part of the number and \(b\) is called the imaginary part of the number. Importantly, \(a\) and \(b\) can be zero, consequently all real numbers are technically complex numbers. We can display complex numbers in the complex plane (also known as the Argand plane) as vectors. Specifically, the \(y\) component of this vector is the imaginary part of our number and the \(x\) component is the real part of the number.

Figure shows the number \(2+3i\) displayed in the complex plane. The length of the vector, \(l\), can be calculated using the Pythagorean theorem as \(l=\sqrt{2^2+3^2}=\sqrt{13}\). \(l\) can also be called the modulus of our complex number. As for \(\theta\), the angle from the positive \(x\)-axis measure counterclockwise it can be found using the definition of the tangent function as \(\theta =\tan^{-1}{\dfrac{3}{2}}\). Note also that \(3=l\sin{\theta}\) and \(2=l\cos{\theta}\) by the definition of these trigonometric functions.

Interestingly, these two qualities, the length of the complex number's vector and its angle, measured counterclockwise from the positive \(x\)-axis, can be used to uniquely define it. To see how, I must first share with you an interesting quality of the exponential function. Namely, that:

\[\begin{aligned} e^{iz} &= \cos{z} + i\sin{z}. \end{aligned}\]
This is known as Euler's formula. Why does this formula hold? Replacing \(x\) with \(iz\) in Equation (5) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} e^{iz} &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(iz)^n}{n!}. \end{aligned}\]
You may be thinking, so what? Well, powers of \(i\) have interesting properties. Namely, \(i^0=1\), \(i^1=i\), \(i^2=-1\), \(i^3=-i\) and \(i^4=1\). Generally, \(i^{4n}\) (where \(n\) is an integer) is equal to \(1\), \(i^{4n+1}\) is \(i\), \(i^{4n+2}\) is \(-1\) and \(i^{4n+3}\) is \(-i\). Focusing on even powers, we find that \(i^{2n}=(-1)^n\) (where makes sense as \(i^2=-1\) and \(i^{2n}=(i^2)^n\)). Whereas odd powers yields \(i^{2n+1}=(-1)^n i\). Consequently:
\[\begin{aligned} e^{iz} &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n z^{2n}}{(2n)!} + \dfrac{(-1)^n iz^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!} \\ &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n z^{2n}}{(2n)!} + \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n iz^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!} \\ &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n z^{2n}}{(2n)!} + i\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{(-1)^n z^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}. \end{aligned}\]
The first of these sums we can recognize as \(\cos{z}\) whereas the second is \(\sin{z}\). Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} e^{iz} &= \cos{z} + i\sin{z}. \end{aligned}\]
An interesting thing to note that \(e^{iz}\) is that its modulus is one. How do I know this? Well, the modulus is defined as just the length of the complex number's vector in the complex plane, right? So for \(e^{iz}\) that would be:
\[\begin{aligned} l &= \sqrt{\cos^2{z}+\sin^2{z}} \\ &= \sqrt{1} \\ &= 1. \end{aligned}\]
Here I used the trigonometric identity:
\[\begin{align} \cos^2{z}+\sin^2{z}=1 \hspace{0.1cm} \forall z \in \mathbb{R}. \tag{14} \end{align}\]
Where \(\mathbb{R}\) is the set of real numbers. Okay, so getting back to how \(l\) and \(\theta\) can uniquely define a complex number. Well, interestingly you can write any complex number in the form \(le^{i\theta}\). Why? Well, recall that \(e^{i\theta}\) is just \(\cos{\theta}+i\sin{\theta}\). Looking at Figure we can clearly see that the \(x\) component of our vector is just \(l\cos{\theta}\), right? And we can also see that the \(y\) component is just \(l\sin{\theta}\) and if we expand our \(le^{i\theta}\) we get:
\[\begin{aligned} le^{i\theta} &= l\cos{\theta} + il\sin{\theta}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence \(le^{i\theta} = x+iy\). For instance, for the complex number \(2+3i\) we can rewrite it as:
\[\begin{aligned} 2+3i &= \sqrt{13}e^{i\tan^{-1}{\frac{3}{2}}}. \end{aligned}\]

Complex analysis

I have definitely not covered everything within the field of complex numbers. There's a field of maths known as complex analysis where we combine calculus and complex numbers. In it, we learn, among other things, how to integrate along curves (or contours) in the complex plane and that there is an extension of Taylor series to the complex plane called the Laurent series. In it, we write:
\[\begin{align} f(z) &= \sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty} a_n (z-z_0)^n \tag{15} \end{align}\]
where:
\[\begin{align} a_n &= \dfrac{1}{2\pi i} \oint_C \dfrac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}} dz. \tag{16} \end{align}\]
This is a contour integral over a curve \(C\) that includes the point \(z=z_0\). \(z_0\) is analogous to the \(a\) in our Taylor series as it is arbitrary. There's a formula called Cauchy's differentiation formula that says that:
\[\begin{align} f^{(n)}(z_0) &= \dfrac{n!}{2\pi i} \oint_C \dfrac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}} dz, \hspace{0.1cm}n \in \{0, 1, 2, ...\}. \tag{17} \end{align}\]
Where \(f^{(n)}(z_0)\) is the \(n\)th derivative of \(f(z)\) evaluated at \(z=z_0\). Which helps us see how the Laurent series is connected to the Taylor series as for non-negative \(n\), \(a_n = \dfrac{f^{(n)}(z_0)}{n!}\). As for negative \(n\), \(a_n=0\) if \(f(z)\) is defined everywhere in the complex plane. Why do I mention this? Well, I personally consider complex analysis one of the most beautiful fields of mathematics and I thought I would mention it in case you are ever in a position to take a complex analysis class while at university.

Partial derivatives

Partial derivatives are just a straightforward extension of derivatives as you understand them already. The key difference is that they are derivatives of multivariable functions instead of single-variable functions. Further, when you differentiate with respect to one variable, all other variables are kept constant. If you would like to build a graphical image of what the partial derivative is, well recall that the derivative of a single-variable function is just the gradient of that function. Consequently, it is also the gradient of the tangent line to that function. The partial derivative is similar to that. As when we have a function with more than a single independent variable, we have the freedom to choose which variable or variables we wish to determine our function's gradient with respect to.

If you want to visualize this, imagine a surface defined by \(z=f(x,y)\) for example. Because a tangent line is two dimensional and we are in three dimensional space, there can be more than one tangent line to a given point. As when constructing the tangent line, we must answer a question: in which direction do we want our line to be tangential to our surface? It could be tangential in the \(x\) direction or the \(y\) direction, or even in any arbitrary direction in the \(xy\) plane. If we want the tangent line in the \(x\) direction, we will need the partial derivative with respect to \(x\) to get its gradient, whereas if we want the tangent line in the \(y\) direction, we will need the partial derivative with respect to \(y\) to get its gradient. More generally, for an arbitrary direction in the \(xy\) plane we would use the directional derivative as it is called.

Say \(f\) is a function of two variables — \(x\) and \(y\). Then its partial derivative with respect to \(x\) is formally defined as:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x} &\stackrel{\text{def}}{=} \lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{f(x+h, y)-f(x, y)}{h}. \end{aligned}\]

This is analogous to the first principles definition of the derivative you would have learned for standard derivatives. Namely, if we are differentiating \(f(x)\):

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{df}{dx} &= \lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\dfrac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}. \end{aligned}\]
The only difference is that now we also have a variable \(y\) that our function \(f(x,y)\) depends on, but is kept constant when differentiating with respect to \(x\). Say \(f(x,y)=2x^2y\). Then \(\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\) would be:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x} &= \lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{2(x+h)^2y - 2x^2y}{h}. \end{aligned}\]
Did you notice that \(2y\) is a common factor of both terms in our numerator? Let us take it out of our limit altogether, as it is effectively a constant (it does not depend on \(h\), after all).

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x} &= 2y\lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{(x+h)^2-x^2}{h} \end{aligned}\]
Using the binomial theorem, we know that
\[\begin{aligned} (x+h)^2 &= x^2 + 2xh + h^2. \end{aligned}\]

Hence:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x} &= 2y\lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{x^2+2xh + h^2-x^2}{h} \\ &= 2y\lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{2xh + h^2}{h} \\ &= 2y\lim_{h\rightarrow 0} 2x + h \\ &= 2y \cdot 2x \\ &= 4xy. \end{aligned}\]
Okay, so that is the long way of finding out partial derivatives. Fortunately, there is also a faster way. Which is that we apply normal differentiation rules, but any variable we are not differentiating with respect to is treated like it is a constant. So for \(f(x,y)=2x^2y\) we would treat the \(2y\) as just a constant number and only differentiate the \(x^2\). Applying the power rule, we see that \(\dfrac{d}{dx}x^2 = 2x\) and hence:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x} &= 2y \cdot 2x \\ &= 4xy. \end{aligned}\]

Heat equation

One of the simplest partial differential equations is the heat equation. In its simplest form it is:

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{\partial u}{\partial t} &= \alpha \dfrac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2} \tag{18} \end{align}\]
Where \(u(x,t)\) is a function describing the temperature of the one-dimensional object (only one dimensional as the above heat equation only has a single spatial dimension, if we want more spatial dimensions, we will have to add second partial derivatives with respect to those other dimensions, too) we are modelling and \(\alpha\) is some constant. I know this equation may look confusing at first glance, but it is actually saying something rather intuitive to you.

Do you remember from your maths class that the second derivative of a function is its concavity? So if the second derivative is negative, that means our function is concave down — it is leading to some sort of maximum. Whereas if it is positive, the function is concave up, which means it is going up from some minimum. We have the second derivative with respect to space on the right-hand side of Equation (18), so it essentially returns positive if we have a localized colder area and neighbouring hotter points. Whereas it returns negative if we have a really hot point surrounded by cooler points. The left-hand side of Equation (18), however, represents the rate of change of our function in time.

Equation (18) therefore essentially says that if we are in a region where there is a really cold point surrounded by hotter points, the temperature will increase in the short-term for that colder point. Whereas the equation says that the temperature will decrease in a hotter region surrounded by colder points. If you have ever brought something hot out of the oven, or put something of room temperature into the fridge or freezer, this should make intuitive sense to you.

Code Listing solves the heat equation in 3D for a \(100^{\circ}C\) sphere in a \(20^{\circ}C\) room.

Heat equation simulation code. 

#!/usr/bin/env julia

using Pkg

# Install missing packages automatically.
for package in ("FFTW", "GLMakie")
    if !haskey(Pkg.project().dependencies, package)
        Pkg.add(package)
    end
end

using FFTW
import GLMakie
using Printf

# ============================================================
# Configuration
# ============================================================

const T_ROOM = 20.0
const T_BALL = 100.0

const ALPHA = 1e-5
const RADIUS = 0.10

const LX = 1.0
const LY = 1.0
const LZ = 1.0

const NX = 64
const NY = 64
const NZ = 64

const SIMULATION_TIME = 1800.0

const NUMBER_OF_FRAMES = 600
const FRAME_RATE = 20

const OUTPUT_FILE = "heat_equation_3d.gif"

# Smooth transition between the hot region and room temperature.
const TRANSITION_CELLS = 6.0

# RKF45 tolerances.
const RELTOL = 1e-7
const ABSTOL = 1e-6

const INITIAL_DT = 0.05
const MAX_DT = 2.0
const MIN_DT = 1e-9

# Conservative explicit-RK stability restriction.
const STABILITY_FACTOR = 0.50

# ============================================================
# Spatial grid
# ============================================================

dx = LX / NX
dy = LY / NY
dz = LZ / NZ

x = collect((0:NX-1) .* dx .- LX / 2)
y = collect((0:NY-1) .* dy .- LY / 2)
z = collect((0:NZ-1) .* dz .- LZ / 2)

# ============================================================
# Smooth initial hot sphere
# ============================================================

const TRANSITION_WIDTH = TRANSITION_CELLS * max(dx, dy, dz)

function smootherstep(s)
    return s^3 * (10.0 + s * (-15.0 + 6.0 * s))
end

function initial_temperature(r)
    inner_radius = RADIUS - TRANSITION_WIDTH / 2
    outer_radius = RADIUS + TRANSITION_WIDTH / 2

    if r <= inner_radius
        return T_BALL
    elseif r >= outer_radius
        return T_ROOM
    else
        s = (r - inner_radius) / TRANSITION_WIDTH
        weight = 1.0 - smootherstep(s)

        return T_ROOM + (T_BALL - T_ROOM) * weight
    end
end

T = Array{Float64}(undef, NX, NY, NZ)

for k in 1:NZ
    for j in 1:NY
        for i in 1:NX
            r = sqrt(x[i]^2 + y[j]^2 + z[k]^2)
            T[i, j, k] = initial_temperature(r)
        end
    end
end

# ============================================================
# Fourier spectral Laplacian
# ============================================================

kx = 2π .* FFTW.fftfreq(NX, 1 / dx)
ky = 2π .* FFTW.fftfreq(NY, 1 / dy)
kz = 2π .* FFTW.fftfreq(NZ, 1 / dz)

KX = reshape(kx, NX, 1, 1)
KY = reshape(ky, 1, NY, 1)
KZ = reshape(kz, 1, 1, NZ)

lambda = -ALPHA .* (
    KX.^2 .+
    KY.^2 .+
    KZ.^2
)

function heat_rhs(u, lambda)
    return real.(ifft(lambda .* fft(u)))
end

# ============================================================
# RKF45
# ============================================================

function rkf45_step(u, h, lambda)

    k1 = heat_rhs(u, lambda)

    k2 = heat_rhs(
        u .+ h .* (1 / 4) .* k1,
        lambda
    )

    k3 = heat_rhs(
        u .+ h .* (
            (3 / 32) .* k1 .+
            (9 / 32) .* k2
        ),
        lambda
    )

    k4 = heat_rhs(
        u .+ h .* (
            (1932 / 2197) .* k1 .-
            (7200 / 2197) .* k2 .+
            (7296 / 2197) .* k3
        ),
        lambda
    )

    k5 = heat_rhs(
        u .+ h .* (
            (439 / 216) .* k1 .-
            8 .* k2 .+
            (3680 / 513) .* k3 .-
            (845 / 4104) .* k4
        ),
        lambda
    )

    k6 = heat_rhs(
        u .+ h .* (
            -(8 / 27) .* k1 .+
            2 .* k2 .-
            (3544 / 2565) .* k3 .+
            (1859 / 4104) .* k4 .-
            (11 / 40) .* k5
        ),
        lambda
    )

    # Fourth-order estimate.
    u4 = u .+ h .* (
        (25 / 216) .* k1 .+
        (1408 / 2565) .* k3 .+
        (2197 / 4104) .* k4 .-
        (1 / 5) .* k5
    )

    # Fifth-order estimate.
    u5 = u .+ h .* (
        (16 / 135) .* k1 .+
        (6656 / 12825) .* k3 .+
        (28561 / 56430) .* k4 .-
        (9 / 50) .* k5 .+
        (2 / 55) .* k6
    )

    return u5, u4
end

# ============================================================
# Stable timestep restriction
# ============================================================

lambda_max_abs = maximum(abs.(lambda))

dt_stability = STABILITY_FACTOR / lambda_max_abs

@printf(
    "Maximum |lambda| = %.8f s^-1\n",
    lambda_max_abs
)

@printf(
    "RKF45 stability timestep limit = %.8f s\n",
    dt_stability
)

# ============================================================
# Adaptive integration
# ============================================================

function integrate_to!(u, t, target, h, lambda)

    while t < target

        h = min(
            h,
            MAX_DT,
            dt_stability,
            target - t
        )

        if h < MIN_DT
            error(
                "RKF45 timestep fell below MIN_DT at t = $t"
            )
        end

        u5, u4 = rkf45_step(u, h, lambda)

        scale =
            ABSTOL .+
            RELTOL .* max.(abs.(u), abs.(u5))

        error_norm =
            maximum(
                abs.(u5 .- u4) ./ scale
            )

        if error_norm <= 1.0
            u .= u5
            t += h
        end

        factor =
            if error_norm == 0.0
                5.0
            else
                clamp(
                    0.9 * error_norm^(-0.2),
                    0.2,
                    5.0
                )
            end

        h = min(
            MAX_DT,
            dt_stability,
            h * factor
        )
    end

    return t, h
end

# ============================================================
# GLMakie visualisation
# ============================================================

GLMakie.activate!()

fig = GLMakie.Figure(
    size = (1000, 800)
)

ax = GLMakie.Axis3(
    fig[1, 1],
    aspect = (1, 1, 1),
    xlabel = "",
    ylabel = "",
    zlabel = "",
    title = ""
)

# ============================================================
# Temperature colour map
#
# 20 °C is opaque white, matching the background.
# ============================================================

temperature_colormap = [
    GLMakie.RGBAf(1.0, 1.0, 1.0, 1.0),  # 20 °C
    GLMakie.RGBAf(0.05, 0.10, 0.80, 1.0),
    GLMakie.RGBAf(0.0, 0.65, 1.0, 1.0),
    GLMakie.RGBAf(0.0, 0.90, 0.45, 1.0),
    GLMakie.RGBAf(1.0, 0.90, 0.0, 1.0),
    GLMakie.RGBAf(1.0, 0.35, 0.0, 1.0),
    GLMakie.RGBAf(0.85, 0.0, 0.0, 1.0)
]

# Makie expects volume dimensions as two endpoints.
volume_plot = GLMakie.volume!(
    ax,
    (x[1], x[end]),
    (y[1], y[end]),
    (z[1], z[end]),
    T;
    algorithm = :mip,
    colormap = temperature_colormap,
    colorrange = (T_ROOM, T_BALL)
)

# ============================================================
# Colourbar
# ============================================================

GLMakie.Colorbar(
    fig[1, 2],
    volume_plot;
    label = "Temperature (°C)",
    ticks = (
        collect(20.0:10.0:100.0),
        string.(collect(20.0:10.0:100.0))
    )
)

# ============================================================
# Simulation time display
# ============================================================

time_label = GLMakie.Label(
    fig[2, 1],
    "Simulation time: 0.00 s";
    tellwidth = false,
    fontsize = 20
)

# ============================================================
# Animation
# ============================================================

frame_times = collect(
    range(
        0.0,
        SIMULATION_TIME;
        length = NUMBER_OF_FRAMES
    )
)

u = copy(T)

# Keep mutable animation state local to the record callback.
let t = 0.0,
    dt = min(INITIAL_DT, dt_stability)

    GLMakie.record(
        fig,
        OUTPUT_FILE,
        enumerate(frame_times);
        framerate = FRAME_RATE
    ) do (frame_number, target_time)

        if frame_number > 1

            t, dt = integrate_to!(
                u,
                t,
                target_time,
                dt,
                lambda
            )

        end

        # Update the volume with the actual temperature field.
        volume_plot[4] = u

        # Update simulation time.
        elapsed_minutes = floor(Int, target_time / 60)
        remaining_seconds = target_time - 60 * elapsed_minutes
        time_label.text =
            @sprintf(
                "Simulation time: %02d min %04.1f s",
                elapsed_minutes,
                remaining_seconds
            )

        # Console diagnostics.
        Tmin = minimum(u)
        Tmax = maximum(u)

        @printf(
            "Frame %4d/%d | t = %02d min %04.1f s | dt = %.5f s | T range = %.8f–%.8f °C\n",
            frame_number,
            NUMBER_OF_FRAMES,
            elapsed_minutes,
            remaining_seconds,
            dt,
            Tmin,
            Tmax
        )
    end
end

println()
println("Animation written to: $OUTPUT_FILE")

Product notation

When we wish to denote a multi-term product, we can use the product notation \(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n a_i\). This essentially says that we multiply \(a_1\), \(a_2\), \(a_3\) and so on until \(a_n\). It is similar to the summation notation \(\displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^n a_i\), with the difference being that instead of addition signs being between each term we have multiplication signs instead.

For instance, for the product \(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n 2i-1\) we would multiply \(2i-1\) for \(i=1\), \(i=2\), \(i=3\), and so forth up until \(i=n\). If we set \(n=3\), then:

\[\begin{aligned} \displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^3 2i-1 &= (2(1)-1) \times (2(2)-1) \times (2(3)-1) \\ &= 1 \times 3 \times 5 \\ &= 15. \end{aligned}\]

Kronecker delta function

The Kronecker delta function is a function that takes two indices and returns zero if the two indices do not equal and one otherwise. That is:

\[\begin{aligned} \delta_{ij} &= \begin{cases} 0,\hspace{0.1cm}i\neq j \\ 1,\hspace{0.1cm}i = j \end{cases}. \end{aligned}\]

Matrices

Matrices are very important in physics and mathematics. In fact, the first formulation of quantum mechanics to be developed was based on matrices and is also called matrix mechanics. Further, vectors are a type of matrix. General relativity and quantum field theory also make extensive use of tensors, which can be thought of as an extension of matrices. Further, when you fit a line of best fit to a scatter plot this involves matrices.

Basic terminology

Matrices have two potential dimensions — rows and columns. As such the dimensions or size of a matrix is described as being \(m\times n\) where \(m\) is the number of rows and \(n\) is the number of columns. If a matrix has an equal number of rows and columns, it is said to be a square matrix. For instance:

\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{A} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \end{alignedat}\]
is a square matrix of size \(2\times 2\). If we want to refer to a specific element of this matrix, we can use its row number and column number. For instance: \(A_{1,1} = 1\) and \(A_{2,2}=4\).

Vectors are a type of matrix; specifically, they are one-dimensional matrices. Either they only have one row (in which case they are a row vector) or one column (in which case they are a column vector).

Addition/subtraction

Addition and subtraction between matrices is element-wise. So:

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{C} &= \mathbf{A}-\mathbf{B}. \end{aligned}\]
Means that \(\mathbf{C}\) has elements that are equal to the corresponding element of \(\mathbf{A}\) minus the corresponding element in \(\mathbf{B}\). For instance:
\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 5 & 6 \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1-0 & 2-1 \\ 3-5 & 4-6 \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ -2 & -2 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]

Multiplication

If you multiply two matrices, you multiply the elements along the corresponding row of the first matrix by the elements of the corresponding column of the second matrix and sum them up. For instance, say we multiplied \(\mathbf{A}\) by this matrix:

\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{B} &= \begin{bmatrix} 5 & 6 \\ 7 & 8 \end{bmatrix} \end{alignedat}\]
Then:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 \times 5 + 2 \times 7 & 1 \times 6 + 2 \times 8 \\ 3 \times 5 + 4 \times 7 & 3 \times 6 + 4 \times 8 \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \begin{bmatrix} 19 & 22 \\ 43 & 50 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
As you can see the first row first column element of the product matrix was obtained by multiplying the first row of \(\mathbf{A}\) by the first column of \(\mathbf{B}\). Likewise, the second row first column element of the product matrix was obtained by multiplying the second row of \(\mathbf{A}\) by the first column of \(\mathbf{B}\).

Due to how we multiply matrices, the matrix product is only defined if we are multiplying by a \(m\times n\) matrix by a \(n\times p\) matrix. The number of columns of the first matrix must match the number of rows of the second. And the result of this multiplication will be a \(m\times p\) matrix. Further, because of this definition of matrix multiplication, the operation is non-commutative. In other words, the order in which we multiply matrices matters. So that if we left-multiply one matrix, say \(\mathbf{A}\), by another, say \(\mathbf{B}\), this is not the equivalent to right-multiplying \(\mathbf{A}\) by \(\mathbf{B}\). In other words, in general:

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B} \neq \mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}. \end{aligned}\]
Although there are some matrices \(\mathbf{A}\) and \(\mathbf{B}\) that are exceptions to this rule.

Simultaneous equations

We can write a series of linear simultaneous equations using matrices. Specifically, say we wanted to express:

\[\begin{aligned} x + 2y &= 4 \\ 3x + 4y &= 9 \end{aligned}\]
in matrix form. Then we could rewrite it as:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x \\ y \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 9 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
If we denote:

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{x} &= \begin{bmatrix} x \\ y \end{bmatrix} \\ \mathbf{b} &= \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 9 \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}\]
Then our equation can be written as:
\[\begin{align} \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x} &= \mathbf{b}. \tag{19} \end{align}\]
And yes, \(\mathbf{x}\) and \(\mathbf{b}\) are both column vectors. Typically, in these equations \(\mathbf{A}\) is a square matrix. If it is a square matrix, we will get exact solutions to our equation.

If it has more rows than columns, then we have an overdetermined system and our solution for \(\mathbf{x}\) will be approximate. Overdetermined systems arise when we are fitting lines of best fit to bivariate data. In that example, you will notice that some of the scatter plot data will not be exactly on the line of best fit. This is precisely because of the approximate nature of our solution. Underdetermined systems have the opposite issue, as we have more columns than rows. This gives us freedom to choose one or more elements of our solution vector \(\mathbf{x}\).

Inversion

Most square matrices like \(\mathbf{A}\) can be inverted. This is the matrix equivalent to the reciprocal. So if you want to solve the equation: \(3x=5\) you would multiply both sides by \(\dfrac{1}{3}\) because this is the reciprocal of \(3\), the coefficient of \(x\). Whereas to solve \(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\) we could multiply both sides by \(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\), the inverse of \(\mathbf{A}\) to get:

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{A} \mathbf{x} &= \mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{b} \\ \mathbf{I}\mathbf{x} &= \mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{b} \\ \mathbf{x} &= \mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{b}. \end{aligned}\]
What is \(\mathbf{I}\)? It is the so called identity matrix. It is essentially the square matrix equivalent to \(1\). As if you multiply it by any matrix with the required dimensions to multiply with it, multiplication by it should not change the matrix at all. Its elements are ones down the main diagonal and zero in all other elements. For instance, the \(2\times 2\) identity matrix is:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{I}_2 &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
I should also mention that some square matrices have no inverse. This is usually the case when one or more rows can be written as linear combinations of the other rows. For instance:
\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \mathbf{A} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 2 & 4 & 6 \\ 4 & 5 & 6 \end{bmatrix} \end{alignedat}\]
is singular because row 2 is row 1 times two.

It is also possible for a matrix to be invertible, but for inverting the matrix to not be a great idea for another reason. Beyond invertibility, when dealing with a square matrix we also often care about its condition number. A matrix with a large, yet finite condition number may be technically invertible but even small errors (such as due to floating point arithmetic errors) in the elements of that matrix can massively change the contents of its inverse. Whereas a matrix with a condition number close to one should not suffer this problem at all. I should also mention that singular matrices have an infinite condition number.

Gaussian elimination

So how do we invert a matrix? We use something analogous to the elimination method of solving simultaneous equations. Specifically, a technique called Gaussian elimination. We can also adapt this technique to solve matrix equations like Equation (61). To invert \(\mathbf{A}\) we first augment it with \(\mathbf{I}\):

\[\begin{alignedat}{4} [\mathbf{A}|\mathbf{I}] &= \left[ \begin{array}{cc|cc} 1 & 2 & 1 & 0\\ 3 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Our aim in inverting \(\mathbf{A}\) is to turn the left of this augmented matrix into the identity matrix. At this point we can read \(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\) off the right side of the augmented matrix.

Let us multiply row 1 by 2:

\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|cc} 2 & 4 & 2 & 0\\ 3 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Next we will subtract row 1 from row 2 and replace row 1 with it:
\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|cc} 1 & 0 & -2 & 1\\ 3 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Next we will subtract three times row 1 from row 2:
\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|cc} 1 & 0 & -2 & 1\\ 0 & 4 & 6 & -2 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Next we will divide row 2 by four:
\[\begin{alignedat}{4} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|cc} 1 & 0 & -2 & 1\\ 0 & 1 & \dfrac{3}{2} & -\dfrac{1}{2} \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{A}^{-1} &= \begin{bmatrix} -2 & 1 \\ \dfrac{3}{2} & -\dfrac{1}{2} \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]

If we want to solve Equation (61), we use a similar procedure. Except we put \(\mathbf{b}\) in place of \(\mathbf{I}\) when we form the augmented matrix. The aim is still to convert the \(\mathbf{A}\) to \(\mathbf{I}\):

\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|c} 1 & 2 & 4\\ 3 & 4 & 9 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
We will do the same row operations. First we will multiply row 1 by 2 and replace row 1 with it. We will denote this operation as \(2R_1 \rightarrow R_1'\):
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|c} 2 & 4 & 8\\ 3 & 4 & 9 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
\(R_2 - R_1 \rightarrow R_1'\):
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|c} 1 & 0 & 1\\ 3 & 4 & 9 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
\(R_2 - 3R_1 \rightarrow R_2'\):
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|c} 1 & 0 & 1\\ 0 & 4 & 6 \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
\(\dfrac{1}{4}R_2 \rightarrow R_2'\):
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \left[ \begin{array}{cc|c} 1 & 0 & 1\\ 0 & 1 & \dfrac{3}{2} \end{array} \right]. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{x} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \dfrac{3}{2} \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}\]

Determinant

Another matrix concept I should probably cover with you is called the determinant. It is a single number value you can obtain from the elements of a square matrix. For a matrix of size \(2\times 2\) it is defined as:

\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \det\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{vmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{vmatrix} \\ &= ad-bc. \end{alignedat}\]
It gets more complex with bigger matrices. For instance, for a \(3\times 3\) matrix it is:
\[\begin{alignedat}{7} \det\begin{bmatrix} A_{1,1} & A_{1,2} & A_{1,3} \\ A_{2,1} & A_{2,2} & A_{2,3} \\ A_{3,1} & A_{3,2} & A_{3,3} \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{vmatrix} A_{1,1} & A_{1,2} & A_{1,3} \\ A_{2,1} & A_{2,2} & A_{2,3} \\ A_{3,1} & A_{3,2} & A_{3,3} \end{vmatrix} \\ &= A_{1,1} \begin{vmatrix} A_{2,2} & A_{2,3} \\ A_{3,2} & A_{3,3} \end{vmatrix} - A_{1,2}\begin{vmatrix} A_{2,1} & A_{2,3} \\ A_{3,1} & A_{3,3} \end{vmatrix} + A_{1,3}\begin{vmatrix} A_{2,1} & A_{2,2} \\ A_{3,1} & A_{3,2} \end{vmatrix} \\ &= A_{1,1}(A_{2,2}A_{3,3}-A_{2,3}A_{3,2}) - A_{1,2}(A_{2,1}A_{3,3}-A_{2,3}A_{3,1}) + A_{1,3}(A_{2,1}A_{3,2}-A_{2,2}A_{3,1}). \end{alignedat}\]
If the determinant is zero, the matrix will be singular. This is because the inverse of a square matrix can be calculated in terms of the reciprocal of the determinant.

Eigenvectors and eigenvalues

If you have the matrix equation:

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{A} \mathbf{x} &= \lambda \mathbf{x} \end{aligned}\]
then \(\lambda\) is said to be an eigenvalue of \(\mathbf{A}\) and \(\mathbf{x}\) is an eigenvector of \(\mathbf{A}\). A square matrix of dimensions \(n\times n\) will have \(n\) distinct eigenvalues and eigenvectors. Each eigenvector will be of dimensions \(n\times 1\). Interestingly, each eigenvector of a matrix is orthogonal to the others. In other words, they are perpendicular to each other — the angle between them is \(90^{\circ}\). This also means that their dot product will be zero.

We solve for \(\lambda\) by solving the polynomial equation that results from:

\[\begin{aligned} \det{\left(\mathbf{A}-\lambda \mathbf{I}\right)} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(\det{\left(\mathbf{A}-\lambda \mathbf{I}\right)}\) denotes the determinant of matrix \(\mathbf{A}-\lambda \mathbf{I}\) and \(\mathbf{I}\) is the identity matrix. Then we figure out \(\mathbf{x}\) by solving:
\[\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{A}-\lambda\mathbf{I})\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}. \end{aligned}\]
When we solve for the eigenvectors, we will find that one unknown is left and this means that the scaling of the vector is arbitrary. Often we choose the scaling to make it so that \(\mathbf{x} \cdot \mathbf{x} = 1\). When this condition is satisfied we say that our eigenvectors are normalized. Say, for example, we have the matrix:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{A} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence the determinant equation is:
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} \det{\left(\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix}-\lambda \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}\right)} &= 0 \\ \det{\left(\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \lambda & 2 \\ 3 & 4 - \lambda \end{bmatrix}\right)} &= 0 \\ \begin{vmatrix} 1 - \lambda & 2 \\ 3 & 4-\lambda \end{vmatrix} & = 0 \\ (1-\lambda)(4-\lambda)-2\times 3 &= 0 \\ 4 - 4\lambda - 1 \lambda + \lambda^2 -6 &= 0 \\ -2-5\lambda + \lambda^2 &= 0 \\ \lambda^2 -5 \lambda - 2 &= 0 \end{alignedat}\]
Next we will use the completing the square technique to solve our quadratic equation:
\[\begin{aligned} \lambda^2 -5 \lambda + \left(\dfrac{5}{2}\right)^2 - \left(\dfrac{5}{2}\right)^2- 2 &= 0 \\ \left(\lambda-\dfrac{5}{2}\right)^2 - \dfrac{25}{4} - 2 &= 0 \\ \left(\lambda-\dfrac{5}{2}\right)^2 - \dfrac{33}{4} &= 0 \\ \left(\lambda-\dfrac{5}{2}\right)^2 &= \dfrac{33}{4} \\ \lambda - \dfrac{5}{2} &= \pm \dfrac{\sqrt{33}}{2} \\ \lambda &= \dfrac{5}{2} \pm \dfrac{\sqrt{33}}{2} \\ &= \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence to find the eigenvectors we need to solve:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2} & 2 \\ 3 & 4-\dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2} \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} a \\ b \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} \\ \begin{bmatrix} \left(1 - \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)a + 2b \\ 3a + \left(4 - \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)b \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
It might seem like we need to solve two equations here, but with eigenvector problems for \(2\times2\) matrices the two equations end up containing the same information. So in this case we only need to solve one.
\[\begin{aligned} \left(1 - \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)a + 2b &= 0 \\ b &= -\dfrac{\left(1 - \dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)a}{2} \\ &= \dfrac{\left(\dfrac{5\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)a-1}{2}. \end{aligned}\]
So we are left to choose what value of \(a\) we want. Say we choose \(a=1\) for the sake of simplicity:
\[\begin{aligned} b &= \dfrac{\left(\dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{2}\right)}{2} \\ &= \dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence our eigenvectors are:
\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{x} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4} \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned}\]
We could, at this point, scale our eigenvectors so that its inner product with itself is one. This is called normalization.

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{x} \cdot \mathbf{x} &= 1^2 + \left(\dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4}\right)^2 \\ &= 1 + \left(\dfrac{3}{4}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{\sqrt{33}}{4}\right)^2 \pm 2 \cdot \dfrac{3}{4} \cdot \dfrac{\sqrt{33}}{4} \\ &= 1 + \dfrac{9}{16} + \dfrac{33}{16} \pm \dfrac{3\sqrt{33}}{8} \\ &= 1 + \dfrac{42}{16} \pm \dfrac{3\sqrt{33}}{8} \\ &= \dfrac{58}{16} \pm \dfrac{3\sqrt{33}}{8} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{16} \left(58\pm 6\sqrt{33}\right). \end{aligned}\]
Hence our normalized eigenvectors, which we will denote as \(\mathbf{\hat{x}}\) would be:
\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{\hat{x}} &= \dfrac{\mathbf{x}}{\sqrt{\mathbf{x}\cdot \mathbf{x}}} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{16} \left(58\pm 6\sqrt{33}\right)}} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{4}{\sqrt{58\pm 6\sqrt{33}}} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \dfrac{3\pm \sqrt{33}}{4} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{58\pm 6\sqrt{33}}} \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 3\pm \sqrt{33} \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}\]

Newton's method

Given we do later cover the Newton-Kantorovich method, which is closely related to Newton's method, I thought I should probably cover Newton's method here. Newton's method, which is also known as the Newton-Raphson method, is a method for solving nonlinear algebraic equations, including systems of nonlinear algebraic equations. For instance, say we wanted to solve:

\[\begin{aligned} x = \cos^2{x}. \end{aligned}\]
Then Newton's method would provide us a way to do so. Newton's method essentially finds the root of a function by looking at where its tangent line intersects the \(x\) axis. Another way to think about it is that it uses the first two terms (the constant term and the linear term) of the function's Taylor series to determine its \(x\) intercept.

For our example, we would rearrange it to make it a function whose root we are finding, namely:

\[\begin{aligned} f(x) &= x -\cos^2{x} \\ &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Then Newton's method says we can apply this scheme to find its zero:
\[\begin{aligned} x_{n+1}=x_n - \dfrac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(x_n\) is our \(n\)th approximation to \(x\). \(x_0\) will be our first guess as to \(f(x)\)'s root. Newton's method will not always converge towards a solution if our initial guess is bad enough. Getting back to our example root-finding problem, I would guess \(x\in[0,1]\) would be where we would find the root for \(f(x)\). \(\cos^2{x}\) is always between \(0\) and \(1\) for all real \(x\), hence \(x\in[0,1]\) sounds like a viable solution. Let us try \(x=1\) as our first guess. Differentiating \(f(x)\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} f'(x) &= 1+2\sin{x}\cos{x} \\ &= 1+\sin{2x}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence Newton's method would come down to:
\[\begin{aligned} x_{n+1} &= x_n - \dfrac{x_n-\cos^2{x_n}}{1+\sin{2x_n}}. \end{aligned}\]
For \(n=0\) we get:
\[\begin{aligned} x_1 &= 1 - \dfrac{1-\cos^2{1}}{1+\sin{2}} \\ &\approx 0.6291445. \end{aligned}\]
For \(n=1\) and \(n=2\), we get:
\[\begin{aligned} x_2 &= x_1 - \dfrac{x_1-\cos^2{x_1}}{1+\sin{2x_1}} \\ &= 0.6417386 \\ x_3 &= x_2 - \dfrac{x_2-\cos^2{x_2}}{1+\sin{2x_2}} \\ &= 0.6417144. \end{aligned}\]
If we continue further iterations of Newton's method, we will find that \(x_n\) stays \(0.6417144\) when rounding to seven decimal places.

How do we extend this method to solving a system of algebraic equations? We use (Boyd, 2000, p. 527):

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{x}_{n+1} &= \mathbf{x}_n - \mathbf{J}_n^{-1}\mathbf{f}_n. \end{aligned}\]
\(\mathbf{x}_{n}\) is a column vector of our \(n\)th approximation to the independent variables that our system of equations depends on. \(\mathbf{f}_n\) is our system of equations written as a vector of functions we are simultaneously setting to zero evaluated at \(\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_n\). \(\mathbf{J}_n\) is the Jacobian matrix evaluated at \(\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_n\). Let the elements of \(\mathbf{x}_n\) and \(\mathbf{f}_n\) be numbered \(1\) to \(m\) (where \(m\) is the number of equations we are solving). What is the Jacobian matrix? Well, it is a matrix with elements (Boyd, 2000, p. 526):
\[\begin{alignedat}{11} \mathbf{J} &= \begin{bmatrix} \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_1} & \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_2} & \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_3} & ... & \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_m} \\ \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_1} & \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_2} & \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_3} & ... & \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_m} \\ \dfrac{\partial f_3}{\partial x_1} & \dfrac{\partial f_3}{\partial x_2} & \dfrac{\partial f_3}{\partial x_3} & ... & \dfrac{\partial f_3}{\partial x_m} \\ ... & ... & ... & ... & ... \\ \dfrac{\partial f_m}{\partial x_1} & \dfrac{\partial f_m}{\partial x_2} & \dfrac{\partial f_m}{\partial x_3} & ... & \dfrac{\partial f_m}{\partial x_m} \\ \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
Which can be written more succinctly as:
\[\begin{aligned} J_{ij} = \left(\dfrac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_j}\right). \end{aligned}\]

For example, say we have the equations:

\[\begin{aligned} xy &= \log{(x+y)} \\ x^2 + y^2 = 9. \end{aligned}\]
First we would rewrite these equations to set one side of them to zero:
\[\begin{aligned} f_1(x,y) &= xy - \log{(x+y)} \\ &= 0 \\ f_2(x,y) &= x^2 + y^2 - 9 \\ &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Hence \(\mathbf{f}\) is:
\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{f} &= \begin{bmatrix} xy - \log{(x+y)} \\ x^2 + y^2 - 9 \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}\]
And our Jacobian is:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{J} &= \begin{bmatrix} \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial x} & \dfrac{\partial f_1}{\partial y} \\ \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial x} & \dfrac{\partial f_2}{\partial y} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \begin{bmatrix} y - \dfrac{1}{x_n+y_n} & x- \dfrac{1}{x_n+y_n} \\ 2x & 2y \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence Newton's method boils down to applying:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{x}_{n+1} &= \mathbf{x}_n - \mathbf{J}_n^{-1}\mathbf{f} \\ \begin{bmatrix} x_{n+1} \\ y_{n+1} \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} x_n \\ y_n \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} y_n - \dfrac{1}{x_n+y_n} & x_n- \dfrac{1}{x_n+y_n} \\ 2x_n & 2y_n \end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix} x_n y_n - \log{(x_n+y_n)} \\ x_n^2 + y_n^2 - 9 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
We can rewrite \(y\) in terms of \(x\) in the second equation, yielding:
\[\begin{aligned} y &= \pm \sqrt{9-x^2}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence \(x\in [-3,3]\) and \(y\in [-3,3]\). \(x+y>0\) thanks the logarithm in the first equation, hence \(y>-x\). Let us choose \(x=2\) and \(y=+\sqrt{9-2^2}=\sqrt{5}\) as a first guess.

Newton's method solution to $\mathbf{f}=\mathbf{0}$ with initial guess $x=2$ and $y=\sqrt{5}$. 

using LinearAlgebra

function newton_system()
    # Initial guesses
    x = 2.0
    y = sqrt(5)

    # Iteration parameters
    tol = 1e-12
    maxiter = 100

    for n in 1:maxiter
        s = x + y

        # Residual vector
        F = [
            x*y - log(s),
            x^2 + y^2 - 9
        ]

        # Jacobian matrix
        J = [
            y - 1/s   x - 1/s
            2x        2y
        ]

        # Newton correction
        Δ = J \ F

        # Update solution
        x -= Δ[1]
        y -= Δ[2]

        # Correction magnitude
        iteration_error = norm(Δ, Inf)

        println("Iteration $n: correction magnitude = $iteration_error")

        if iteration_error < tol
            println("\nNewton iteration converged.")
            break
        end

        if n == maxiter
            @warn "Newton iteration did not converge."
        end
    end

    println("\nNumerical solution:")
    println("x = $x")
    println("y = $y")

    return x, y
end

x, y = newton_system()
Iteration 1: correction magnitude = 7.171592071160942
Iteration 2: correction magnitude = 3.3846060587446156
Iteration 3: correction magnitude = 1.558372734342074
Iteration 4: correction magnitude = 0.6015046278196632
Iteration 5: correction magnitude = 0.1278352945181429
Iteration 6: correction magnitude = 0.00634735828336772
Iteration 7: correction magnitude = 1.5726214269007225e-5
Iteration 8: correction magnitude = 9.653699887703496e-11
Iteration 9: correction magnitude = 3.006347332366074e-16

Newton iteration converged.

Numerical solution:
x = 0.4099865626018238
y = 2.971853128686871

As we can see above, \(x=2\) and \(y=\sqrt{5}\) are actually pretty poor first guesses, but our solution still luckily converged.

Differential equations

As this article involves a lot of differential equations, I thought I should cover a little on how we categorize and solve differential equations. This is definitely not intended to be a comprehensive covering of this topic. As this is a topic so broad that entire textbooks are written about it.

Differential equations are equations that involve the derivatives of a function and typically the aim in solving them is to determine the original function they pertain to. For instance, say you had the uncoupled, linear, homogeneous, first-order ordinary differential equation:

\[\begin{align} y' &= y. \tag{20} \end{align}\]
Our aim would be to determine what \(y\) is.

I also showed you this equation to clarify some of the terminology we use with regards to differential equations. So why is this equation uncoupled? Because it is a single equation and not a series of differential equations we need to solve simultaneously. Why is this equation linear? Well, it is because there is no term that is nonlinear in \(y\) or its derivative(s). For instance, if the equation was \(y'=y^2\), that would make it nonlinear because the right-hand side is nonlinear in \(y\). Why is it an ordinary differential equation (ODE)? Because there are only ordinary derivatives, not partial derivatives. Why is it homogeneous? Because there are no terms that do not involve \(y\) or its derivative(s). Why is it first order? Because the highest order derivative in the equation is the first derivative.

I should also tell you that because differentiating deletes constants from our function, in order to obtain a unique solution to a given differential equation of order \(n\), we need \(n\) additional conditions beyond the differential equation itself. Often these conditions are initial conditions, that is they specify the initial state of our system. In this event, our differential equation is called an initial value problem. For instance, if we wanted to study \(\ddot{x}=-g\) over \(t \in [0, 10]\) then the appropropriate initial conditions could be \(x(0)=50\) and \(\dot{x}(0)=0\), as these specify the initial state of our system.

Other conditions are possible, too. For instance, boundary conditions could also be used for our ODE \(\ddot{x}=-g\). As the term suggests, boundary conditions involve the values of our function and/or its derivatives at the endpoints (or boundary) of our domain. For this problem, the appropriate boundary conditions could be the values of \(x(0)\) and \(x(10)\). In this case, we would call the problem a boundary value problem. We could also have the conditions \(\dot{x}(2)=50\) and \(x(5)=10\), which together do not really fit neatly into the definitions of either initial or boundary conditions.

Sturm-Liouville problems

Sturm-Liouville problems, in general, are of the form (Trench, 2024) :

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d}{dx}\left[p(x) \dfrac{dy}{dx}\right] + q(x)y &= -\lambda r(x) y. \tag{21} \end{align}\]
Where \(p>0\), \(r\geq 0\). There are also boundary conditions imposed as part of a Sturm-Liouville problem, namely (Lebl, 2025) :
\[\begin{aligned} \alpha_1 y(a) -\alpha_2 y'(a) &= 0 \\ \beta_1 y(b) + \beta_2 y'(b) &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(\alpha_1\) and \(\alpha_2\) cannot both be simultaneously zero and nor can \(\beta_1\) and \(\beta_2\). \(\lambda\) can take on a set of discrete values, which are called eigenvalues. Each eigenvalue corresponds to a different solution \(y\) which is called the corresponding eigenfunction. We usually order the eigenvalues, and the corresponding eigenfunctions, by the absolute value of the eigenvalue in ascending order. So the first eigenvalue is the one with the smallest absolute value and the first eigenfunction is the eigenfunction corresponding to the eigenvalue with the lowest absolute value.

An interesting quality of eigenfunctions is that they are orthogonal (similarly to the eigenvectors of a matrix). That means that:

\[\begin{aligned} \langle y_m | y_n \rangle &= \int_a^b y_m y_n r(x) dx \\ &= c_m \delta_{mn}. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(c_m\) is some constant and \(\langle y_m | y_n \rangle\) is called the inner product between \(y_m\) and \(y_n\). Interestingly, the dot product between two vectors is also referred to as the inner product. Just like the function inner product, the dot product also yields zero if two vectors are orthogonal. There are a lot of parallels between Sturm-Liouville theory and the study of matrices and vectors.

Rearranging and integrating

For Equation (20), there would be a few ways we could solve this differential equation. As luckily each of the adjectives we used to describe it implies it should be easy to solve. One option is we could divide both sides by \(y\) and integrate on \(x\):

\[\begin{aligned} \int \dfrac{\dfrac{dy}{dx}}{y} dx &= \int dx \\ \int \dfrac{dy}{y} &= \int dx \\ \ln{y} &= x + C \end{aligned}\]
Exponentiating both sides yields:
\[\begin{aligned} y &= e^{x+C}. \end{aligned}\]
Note that we have one unknown constant, \(C\). This is a product of how derivatives delete constants and is why we need initial, boundary or other conditions to uniquely determine our solution. For instance, if \(y(0)=1\), then we can substitute this into our solution to find \(C\).
\[\begin{aligned} 1 &= e^{0+C} \\ e^C &= 1 \\ \implies C &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} y &= e^x. \end{aligned}\]

Power series method

Another method to solve this and many other linear ODEs is to assume a power series expansion of \(y\):

\[\begin{aligned} y(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n x^n \\ \therefore y'(x) &= \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} a_n nx^{n-1}. \end{aligned}\]
We ommitted \(n=0\) from the \(y'(x)\) power series because the derivative of \(x^0=1\) is zero. We can rewrite the derivative power series as:
\[\begin{aligned} y'(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_{n+1} (n+1) x^n. \end{aligned}\]
Hence our differential equation becomes:
\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_{n+1} (n+1) x^n &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n x^n. \end{aligned}\]
Equating the coefficients of \(x^n\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} a_{n+1}(n+1) &= a_n. \end{aligned}\]
Dividing by \(n+1\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} a_{n+1} &= \dfrac{a_n}{n+1} \end{aligned}\]
Applying this recursive relationship over and over yields:
\[\begin{aligned} a_{n+1} &= \dfrac{a_{n-1}}{(n+1)n} \\ &= \dfrac{a_{n-2}}{(n+1)n(n-1)} \\ &= \dfrac{a_0}{(n+1)\times n\times(n-1)\times...\times1} \\ &= \dfrac{a_0}{(n+1)!}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the coefficients of our power series are:
\[\begin{aligned} a_n &= \dfrac{a_0}{n!}. \end{aligned}\]
Then using our knowledge of Maclaurin series, we know that \(a_0\) must be \(y(0)\). \(y(0)=1\) and hence:
\[\begin{aligned} a_n &= \dfrac{1}{n!}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} y &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{x^n}{n!}. \end{aligned}\]
Which we can recognize as the Maclaurin series of \(e^x\). I guess we could have more directly used our knowledge of Maclaurin series and the fact that \(y'=y\) implies that:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d^n y}{dx^n} = y. \end{aligned}\]
Hence \(y(0)=1\) implies that:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d^n y}{dx^n} \Big|_{x=0} &= 1 \end{aligned}\]
and hence:
\[\begin{aligned} y(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \dfrac{x^n}{n!}. \end{aligned}\]

Assuming an exponential solution

Another method we could use for this sort of ODE is only applicable to ODEs with constant coefficients. That is, ODEs of the form:

\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i \dfrac{d^i y}{dx^i} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(a_i\) are constant numbers. In this method, we assume that:
\[\begin{aligned} y &= \sum_{j=1}^{n} C_j e^{r_j x}. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(C_j\) is a constant that depends on our initial conditions and \(r_j\) depends on our equation. Susbstituting into our ODE yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=0}^{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_i C_j r_j^i e^{r_j x} &= 0 \\ \sum_{j=1}^{n} C_j e^{r_j x}\sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i r_j^i &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Hence we have to solve:
\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i r_j^i &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
for \(j\in \{1,2,3,...,n\}\). This is really just a polynomial equation of degree \(n\) that we need to solve and \(r_j\) is just the \(j\)th solution to this equation. For \(y'=y\), for instance \(a_0=-1\) and \(a_1=1\) and hence we need to solve:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} -r_j^0 + r_j &= 0 \implies r_j &= 1. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence our solution is:
\[\begin{aligned} y(x) &= \sum_{j=1}^1 C_j e^{x} \\ &= C_1 e^x. \end{aligned}\]
And with \(y(0)=1\) we obtain \(C_1=1\). Hence \(y(x)=e^x\). To see how it works, let us try to solve \(y'''+6y''+11y'+6y=0\). Hence our polynomial equation is:
\[\begin{aligned} r_j^3 + 6r_j^2 + 11r_j + 6 = 0. \end{aligned}\]
Substituting \(r_j=-1\) into the left-hand side of this equation yields \(0\). Hence \(r_j+1\) is a factor. Using synthetic division (polynomial long division will work equally well) we get:
\[\begin{alignedat}{6} \begin{array}{cc} \begin{array}{r} \\ -1 \\ \end{array} & \begin{array}{|rrrr} \ 1 & 6 & 11 & 6 \\ & -1 & -5 & -6 \\ \hline 1 & 5 & 6 & 0 \end{array} \end{array}. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence our polynomial equation becomes:
\[\begin{aligned} (r_j+1)(r_j^2+5r_j+6) &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
3 and 2 multiply to give 6 and add to give 5, hence the quadratic factor can be factorized as \((r_j+2)(r_j+3)\):
\[\begin{aligned} (r_j+1)(r_j+2)(r_j+3) &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Applying the null factor law and rearranging yields:
\[\begin{aligned} r_1 &= -1 \\ r_2 &= -2 \\ r_3 &= -3. \end{aligned}\]

Hence \(y(x) = C_1 e^{-x} + C_2 e^{-2x} + C_3 e^{-3x}\) where \(C_1\), \(C_2\) and \(C_3\) depend on our initial conditions.

Separation of variables

I thought I should cover one technique for solving partial differential equations, too. Say we had the heat equation, Equation (18). Then, if we are using the separation of variables technique, we would assume that \(u(x,t)\) can be written as \(u(x,t)=X(x)T(t)\). Then Equation (18) becomes:

\[\begin{aligned} X(x)\dot{T}(t) &= \alpha X''(x)T(t). \end{aligned}\]
Dividing both sides by \(u(x,t)\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\dot{T}(t)}{T(t)} &= \alpha \dfrac{X''(x)}{X(x)}. \end{aligned}\]
The left-hand side depends only on \(t\) and the right-hand side only depends on \(x\). Hence both sides must equal a constant, which we will call \(-\lambda\). The importance of the negative sign will become apparent soon. Hence we can write a pair of ordinary differential equations we need to solve. For \(T(t)\), the equation is:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\dot{T}(t)}{T(t)} &= -\lambda \\ \dot{T}(t) &= -\lambda T(t) \end{aligned}\]
As for \(X(x)\), its equation is:
\[\begin{aligned} \alpha \dfrac{X''(x)}{X(x)} &= -\lambda \\ \alpha X''(x) &= -\lambda X(x) \\ X''(x) &= -\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha} X(x). \end{aligned}\]
These are both linear, homogeneous differential equations with constant coefficients. Hence, our solutions will involve exponential functions. Solving the polynomial equations we get from this knowledge yields:
\[\begin{align} T(t) &= C_1 e^{-\lambda t} \tag{22} \\ X(x) &= C_2 e^{i\sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}x} + C_3 e^{-i\sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}x}. \tag{23} \end{align}\]
\(C_1\), \(C_2\) and \(C_3\) are constants that depend on our boundary and initial conditions. Since \(e^{i\theta}=\cos{\theta}+i\sin{\theta}\):
\[\begin{align} X(x) &= C_2 \cos{\sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}x} + C_3 \sin{\sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}x}. \tag{24} \end{align}\]
So this is why we had opted to give \(\lambda\) a negative sign earlier. As real exponentials will not satisfy our boundary conditions of \(X(0)=X(L)=0\) without \(C_2\) and \(C_3\) both being zero. The \(C_2\) and \(C_3\) we used in Equation (24) will be different to those in Equation (23). Then we apply our boundary and initial conditions, which are often of the form:
\[\begin{aligned} u(x,0)&=f(x) \\ u(0,t)&=0 \\ u(L,t)&=0. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(x\in [0,L]\) is our spatial domain and \(f(x)\) is some specified function. We will impose our spatial boundary conditions on \(X(x)\), \(X(0)=0\) implies that:
\[\begin{aligned} C_2 \cos{0} + C_3\sin{0} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
\(\sin{0}=0\), \(\cos{0}=1\) and hence:
\[\begin{aligned} C_2 &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
As for our other boundary condition:
\[\begin{aligned} X(L) &= 0 \\ C_3\sin{\sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}L} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
This, in turn, implies that:
\[\begin{aligned} \sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha}}L &= n\pi,\hspace{0.2cm}n\in \mathbb{Z}. \end{aligned}\]
Squaring both sides yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\lambda}{\alpha} L^2 &= n^2 \pi^2 \end{aligned}\]
Multiplying by \(\dfrac{\alpha}{L^2}\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \lambda &= \dfrac{\alpha n^2 \pi^2}{L^2}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence we do not have a just a singular solution for \(\lambda\) and we should really call it \(\lambda_n\) to reflect this reality. \(X(x)\), \(T(t)\) and \(u(x,t)\) all depend on \(\lambda_n\) and hence should get a \(n\) subscript.
\[\begin{aligned} \lambda_n &= \dfrac{\alpha n^2 \pi^2}{L^2} \\ X_n(x) &= C_{3n} \sin{\sqrt{\dfrac{n^2 \pi^2}{L^2} x}} \\ &= C_{3n} \sin{\left(\dfrac{|n| \pi}{L} x\right)} \\ T_n(t) &= C_{1n}e^{-\frac{\alpha n^2 \pi^2}{L^2} t}. \end{aligned}\]
Why have I given the constants \(n\) indices? Because they, too, could vary between terms with different \(\lambda\). Why the \(|n|\)? Well, we square rooted \(n^2\), so it will be the absolute value of \(n\). We will call \(C_{1n}C_{3n}\), \(a_n\) when we write \(u_n(x,t)\): Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} u_n(x,t) &= a_n \sin{\left(\dfrac{|n| \pi}{L} x\right)} e^{-\frac{\alpha n^2 \pi^2}{L^2} t}. \end{aligned}\]
Keep in mind that \(u_{-n}(x,t)=u_n(x,t)\) and that \(u_0=0\) (as \(\sin{0}=0\)), hence we only need to sum over \(n \in \mathbb{Z}^{+}\). Hence, we obtain the following series as our solution:
\[\begin{aligned} u(x,t) &= \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} a_n \sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)} e^{-\frac{n^2 \pi^2}{L^2} t}. \end{aligned}\]
Imposing that \(u(x,0)=f(x)\) yields:
\[\begin{align} f(x) &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n \sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)}. \tag{25} \end{align}\]
How can we determine \(a_n\) from this? Let us investigate what happens if we multiply this by \(\sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)}\) and integrate over \(x\in [0,L]\):
\[\begin{aligned} \int_0^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)} dx &= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n \int_0^L\sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)}\sin{\left(\dfrac{m \pi}{L} x\right)} dx. \end{aligned}\]
Interestingly:
\[\begin{aligned} \sin{A}\sin{B} &= \dfrac{1}{2}(\cos{(A-B)-\cos{(A+B)}}). \end{aligned}\]
This yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)}\sin{\left(\dfrac{m \pi}{L} x\right)} &= \dfrac{1}{2}\left(\cos{\left(\dfrac{(m-n)\pi}{L}x\right)} - \cos{\left(\dfrac{(m+n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right). \end{aligned}\]
Hence our integral becomes:
\[\begin{aligned} \int_0^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{m \pi}{L} x\right)} dx &= \dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n \int_0^L \left(\cos{\left(\dfrac{(m-n)\pi}{L}x\right)} - \cos{\left(\dfrac{(m+n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right) dx \\ &= \dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n \left(\left[\dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{\left(\dfrac{(m-n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right]_0^L - \left[\dfrac{L}{(m+n)\pi}\sin{\left(\dfrac{(m+n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right]_0^L\right) \\ &= \dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n \left(\dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{((m-n)\pi)}-\dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{0} - \left[\dfrac{L}{(m+n)\pi}\sin{((m+n)\pi)}-\dfrac{L}{(m+n)\pi}\sin{0}\right]\right). \end{aligned}\]
\(m+n\) and \(m-n\) is always an integer so the sines should be zero. \(\sin{0}=0\), too. Hence, we will get \(0\) from this if \(m\neq n\). If \(m=n\) though, we end up dividing by 0 for the first term, which means we cannot simply declare it zero. So we will need to take the limit as \(m\) approaches \(n\) to determine the value of the first integral at its upper bound:
\[\begin{aligned} \lim_{m\rightarrow n} \dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{\left(\dfrac{(m-n)\pi}{L}L\right)} &= \lim_{m\rightarrow n} \dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{(m-n)\pi}. \end{aligned}\]
Let \(x=(m-n)\pi\):
\[\begin{aligned} \lim_{m\rightarrow n} \dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{\left((m-n)\pi\right)} &= \lim_{x\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{L}{x} \sin{x}. \end{aligned}\]
We can use the first term of the Maclaurin series to approximate \(\sin{x}\) as \(x\rightarrow 0\):
\[\begin{aligned} \lim_{x\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{L}{x} \sin{x} &= \lim_{x\rightarrow 0} \dfrac{L}{x} x \\ &= \lim_{x\rightarrow 0} L \\ &= L. \end{aligned}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} \left[\dfrac{L}{(m-n)\pi}\sin{\left(\dfrac{(m-n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right]_0^L &= L\delta_{mn} \\ \left[\dfrac{L}{(m+n)\pi}\sin{\left(\dfrac{(m+n)\pi}{L}x\right)}\right]_0^L &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} \int_0^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{m \pi}{L} x\right)} dx &= \dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a_n L \delta_{mn} \\ &= \dfrac{La_m}{2}. \end{aligned}\]
Here we have used the fact that \(\delta_{mn}\) is zero if \(m\neq n\) and one if \(m=n\) and hence the only non-zero term corresponds to \(m=n\). Hence we can calculate \(a_n\) as:
\[\begin{aligned} a_n &= \dfrac{2}{L} \int_0^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{n \pi}{L} x\right)} dx. \end{aligned}\]
Interestingly, Equation (25) is called the Fourier series decomposition of \(f(x)\) in terms of sines only. Fourier series are a way of rewriting periodic functions in terms of sine and cosine waves. Specifically, if we wish to write \(f(x)\) as a Fourier series over the domain \(x \in [-L,L]\), we would write (keep in mind the series coefficients \(a_n\) and \(b_n\) are not the same as those we have been using so far):
\[\begin{aligned} f(x) &= a_0 + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} a_n \cos{\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{L}x\right)} + b_n \sin{\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{L}x\right)}. \end{aligned}\]
Where:
\[\begin{aligned} a_0 &= \dfrac{1}{2L} \int_{-L}^{L} f(x) dx \\ a_n &= \dfrac{1}{L} \int_{-L}^{L} f(x) \cos{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \\ b_n &= \dfrac{1}{L} \int_{-L}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx. \end{aligned}\]
We can see our \(b_n\) here matches the \(a_n\) from the \(u_n(x,t)\), except that \(a_n\) is calculated by integrating only over \(x\in [0,L]\). So what is up with this? Well, interestingly we can only represent \(f(x)\) solely with sine terms if and only if \(f(x)\) is an odd function, just like \(\sin{\left(\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}\right)}\) itself. Meaning that \(f(-x)=-f(x)\). Hence we can split our \(b_n\) integral as:
\[\begin{aligned} b_n &= \dfrac{1}{L} \int_{-L}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \\ &= \dfrac{1}{L} \left[\int_{-L}^{0} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx + \int_{0}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \right] \end{aligned}\]
In our first integral, we will replace \(x\) with \(-x\) and hence \(dx\rightarrow-dx\) and our domain becomes \(x\in [L, 0]\) instead of \(x\in [-L, 0]\).
\[\begin{aligned} b_n &= \dfrac{1}{L} \left[\int_{L}^{0} f(-x) \sin{\left(-\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} \cdot -dx + \int_{0}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \right] \\ &= \dfrac{1}{L} \left[\int_{L}^{0} -f(x) \cdot -\sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} \cdot -dx + \int_{0}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \right] \\ &= \dfrac{1}{L} \left[-\int_{L}^{0} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx + \int_{0}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \right] \\ &= \dfrac{1}{L} \left[\int_{0}^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx + \int_{0}^{L} f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx \right] \\ &= \dfrac{2}{L} \int_{0}^L f(x) \sin{\left(\dfrac{2\pi nx}{L}\right)} dx. \end{aligned}\]
So we see that our \(a_n\) from \(u_n(x,t)\) are in fact equivalent to \(b_n\), at least for odd functions.

I should also mention that while Fourier series are technically intended for periodic functions, our function actually does not need to repeat itself within the domain we are calculating it for. Further, how the function behaves outside that domain is irrelevant to the calculation of Fourier series. So the Fourier series can, in fact, match our function perfectly within our domain despite not actually being periodic. Although, this lack of periodicity will cause the Fourier series and the original function to not match outside the domain in which we calculated the series.

Interestingly, our differential equation for \(X_n\) is an example of a Sturm-Liouville problem. \(X_n\) were the eigenfunctions and \(\lambda_n\) were the eigenvalues. The orthogonality quality of eigenfunctions is quite useful to us when we are solving linear partial differential equations using separation of variables and at least one of the equations we get from this is a Sturm-Liouville problem. As it often makes finding the coefficients of the series expansion of our solution easier. Another partial differential equation whose solution gives rise to Sturm-Liouville problems is the Schrödinger equation.

Numerical solution techniques

In this article, however, we will encounter differential equations that have no exact solutions and for which numerical solutions are necessary. Numerical solution methods approximate the solution to a differential equations, and as such they are not exact. Although, quite often we can make them as accurate as we need them to be (even greater than ten significant figures).

The first three methods we will cover — Euler's method, Runge-Kutta methods and Runge-Kutta-Fehlberg 4/5th order method — all are most applicable to initial value problems. After this, we cover matrix methods of solving differential equations such as the finite difference method and spectral and related methods. These methods are most easily applicable to linear differential equations (although, they can be adapted to work with nonlinear differential equations, too). A major advantage they have is that they can work with any conditions we may wish to impose on our differential equation's solution. After these sections, we briefly cover how partial differential equations can be numerically integrated and then cover how we can use the Newton-Kantorovich method to linearize differential equations and thereby apply matrix methods to numerically solving them.

Euler's method
