Hence the \(r_2\) Euler-Lagrange equation is:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d}{dt} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{r}_2} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial r_2} \tag{51} \\ m_2 \ddot{r}_2 &= m_2 r_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 - \dfrac{GMm_2}{r_2^2} - \dfrac{Gm_1 m_2( r_2-r_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})}{r_{12}^3} \notag \\ \ddot{r}_2 &= r_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_2^2} - \dfrac{Gm_1( r_2-r_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})}{r_{12}^3}. \tag{52} \end{align}\]
As for the \(\theta_1\) Euler-Lagrange equation, it is:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d}{dt} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_1} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_1} \\ 2m_1 r_1\dot{r}_1 \dot{\theta}_1 + m_1 r_1^2 \ddot{\theta}_1 &= \dfrac{Gm_1 m_2 r_1r_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Divide both sides by \(m_1r_1^2\):
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{2\dot{r}_1 \dot{\theta}_1}{r_1} + \ddot{\theta}_1 &= \dfrac{Gm_2 r_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_1 r_{12}^3} \notag \\ \ddot{\theta}_1 &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_1 \dot{\theta}_1}{r_1} + \dfrac{Gm_2 r_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_1 r_{12}^3}. \tag{53} \end{align}\]
As for \(\theta_2\):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d}{dt} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_2} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_2} \\ 2m_2 r_2\dot{r}_2 \dot{\theta}_2 + m_2 r_2^2 \ddot{\theta}_2 &= \dfrac{Gm_1 m_2 r_1r_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Dividing both sides by \(m_2r_2^2\):
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{2\dot{r}_2 \dot{\theta}_2}{r_2} + \ddot{\theta}_2 &= -\dfrac{Gm_1 r_1\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_2 r_{12}^3} \notag \\ \ddot{\theta}_2 &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_2 \dot{\theta}_2}{r_2} -\dfrac{Gm_1 r_1\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_2 r_{12}^3}. \tag{54} \end{align}\]

Equations (50), (52), (53) and (54) cannot be analytically solved, unlike the equations we obtained for the two-body problem. Consequently, we are forced to resort to numerical methods to solve this system.

Simulation

Code Listing is Julia code to numerically solve the system for a made-up example of the three-body problem. We assume each of the orbits start out elliptical and \(a\) is their semi-major axis and \(e\) is their eccentricity. These parts of the initial conditions are:

\[\begin{alignedat}{2} a_1 &= 5,&\hspace{0.2cm} a_2 &= 5 \\ e_1 &= 0.15,&\hspace{0.2cm} e_2 &= 0.10. \end{alignedat}\]
We will start the simulation with each of the moving masses at their periapsis. Which is when they are at their closest point to the central mass. From this, we get the following initial conditions:
\[\begin{align} r_i(t_0) &= a_i(1-e_i) \tag{55} \\ \dot{r}_i(t_0) &= 0 \tag{56} \\ v_i(t_0) &= \sqrt{\dfrac{GM(1+e_i)}{r_i(t_0)}} \tag{57} \\ \dot{\theta}_i(t_0) &= \dfrac{v_i(t_0)}{r_i(t_0)}. \tag{58} \end{align}\]
As for \(G\) and the masses:
\[\begin{alignedat}{3} G &= 1,& \hspace{0.2cm} M &= 3,& \hspace{0.2cm} m_1 &= 1.5, \\ m_2 &= 2.0. \end{alignedat}\]
As for the initial angles they are, in units of radians:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \theta_1(t_0) &= 0,& \hspace{0.2cm} \theta_2(t_0) &= \pi - 0.15. \end{alignedat}\]

It produces the animation in Figure .

Three-body problem simulation code. 
using DifferentialEquations
using Plots, Measures, Colors
using Printf, LaTeXStrings
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"
# ============================================================
# Three-body gravitational system
#
# Body M is fixed at the origin.
# Bodies m₁ and m₂ orbit M while also perturbing one another.
#
# Unlike a strongly hierarchical system, the masses here are
# comparable enough for the mutual interaction to produce
# strongly non-Keplerian and potentially chaotic motion.
#
# Units are normalised:
#   G = 1
#   mass = arbitrary mass unit
#   distance = arbitrary distance unit
#   time = corresponding dynamical time unit
# ============================================================

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Parameters
# ------------------------------------------------------------

G              = 1.0
M              = 3.0
m₁             = 1.5
m₂             = 2.0
a₁             = 5.0
e₁             = 0.15
a₂             = 5.0
e₂             = 0.10
θ₁₀            = 0.0
θ₂₀            = π - 0.15
tspan          = (0.0, 200.0)
abstol         = 1e-11
reltol         = 1e-11
fps            = 60
marksize       = 10
stepsize       = 10/fps
legendfontsize = 24
guidefontsize  = 24
tickfontsize   = 20
titlefontsize  = 30

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial approximate Keplerian orbits
# ------------------------------------------------------------

# Periapsis distances

r₁₀ = a₁ * (1 - e₁)
r₂₀ = a₂ * (1 - e₂)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial Keplerian periapsis velocities
# ------------------------------------------------------------

v₁₀ = sqrt(G * M * (1 + e₁) / r₁₀)
v₂₀ = sqrt(G * M * (1 + e₂) / r₂₀)

# Radial velocity is zero at periapsis.

ṙ₁₀ = 0.0
ṙ₂₀ = 0.0

# Since v = r θ̇:

θ̇₁₀ = v₁₀ / r₁₀
θ̇₂₀ = v₂₀ / r₂₀

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial state vector
#
# u = [
#     r₁,
#     r₂,
#     θ₁,
#     θ₂,
#     ṙ₁,
#     ṙ₂,
#     θ̇₁,
#     θ̇₂
# ]
# ------------------------------------------------------------

u₀ = [
    r₁₀,
    r₂₀,
    θ₁₀,
    θ₂₀,
    ṙ₁₀,
    ṙ₂₀,
    θ̇₁₀,
    θ̇₂₀
]

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Equations of motion
# ------------------------------------------------------------

function three_body!(du, u, p, t)

    r₁, r₂, θ₁, θ₂, ṙ₁, ṙ₂, θ̇₁, θ̇₂ = u

    Δθ = θ₂ - θ₁

    r₁₂ = sqrt(
        r₁^2 +
        r₂^2 -
        2*r₁*r₂*cos(Δθ)
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Radial accelerations
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    r̈₁ =
        r₁*θ̇₁^2 -
        G*M/r₁^2 -
        G*m₂*(r₁ - r₂*cos(Δθ))/r₁₂^3

    r̈₂ =
        r₂*θ̇₂^2 -
        G*M/r₂^2 -
        G*m₁*(r₂ - r₁*cos(Δθ))/r₁₂^3

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Angular accelerations
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    θ̈₁ =
        -2*ṙ₁*θ̇₁/r₁ +
        G*m₂*r₂*sin(Δθ)/(r₁*r₁₂^3)

    θ̈₂ =
        -2*ṙ₂*θ̇₂/r₂ -
        G*m₁*r₁*sin(Δθ)/(r₂*r₁₂^3)

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # First-order system
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    du[1] = ṙ₁
    du[2] = ṙ₂
    du[3] = θ̇₁
    du[4] = θ̇₂

    du[5] = r̈₁
    du[6] = r̈₂
    du[7] = θ̈₁
    du[8] = θ̈₂
end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Integrate
#
# 100 time units gives the system ample time to undergo
# several orbital periods and develop strongly perturbed
# trajectories.
# ------------------------------------------------------------

prob = ODEProblem(
    three_body!,
    u₀,
    tspan
)

solut = solve(
    prob,
    Vern8(),
    saveat=stepsize,
    abstol=abstol,
    reltol=reltol
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Convert polar coordinates to Cartesian coordinates
# ------------------------------------------------------------

x₁ = solut[1, :] .* cos.(solut[3, :])
y₁ = solut[1, :] .* sin.(solut[3, :])

x₂ = solut[2, :] .* cos.(solut[4, :])
y₂ = solut[2, :] .* sin.(solut[4, :])

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Calculate body-body separation
# ------------------------------------------------------------

separation = sqrt.(
    (x₂ .- x₁).^2 +
    (y₂ .- y₁).^2
)
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Plot limits
# ------------------------------------------------------------

x_all = vcat(0.0, x₁, x₂)
y_all = vcat(0.0, y₁, y₂)

xmin = minimum(x_all)
xmax = maximum(x_all)
ymin = minimum(y_all)
ymax = maximum(y_all)

span = max(
    xmax - xmin,
    ymax - ymin
)

margin = 0.08 * span

xc = (xmin + xmax) / 2
yc = (ymin + ymax) / 2

xmin = xc - span/2 - margin
xmax = xc + span/2 + margin

ymin = yc - span/2 - margin
ymax = yc + span/2 + margin

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Animation
# ------------------------------------------------------------

m1col = RGB24(0, 0, 155/255)
m1outcol = RGB24(190/255, 190/255, 255/255)
m2col = RGB24(0, 155/255, 155/255)
m2outcol = RGB24(190/255, 255/255, 255/255)
Mcol  = RGB24(65/255, 130/255, 65/255)

anim = @animate for i in eachindex(solut.t)

    p = plot(
        x₁[1:i],
        y₁[1:i],
        linewidth=1,
        color=m1col,
        label=false,
        xlims=(xmin, xmax),
        ylims=(ymin, ymax),
        aspect_ratio=:equal,
        xlabel=L"x",
        ylabel=L"y",
        title="Three-body system — t = $(@sprintf("%.2f", solut.t[i]))",
        guidefontsize=guidefontsize,
        tickfontsize=tickfontsize,
        titlefontsize=titlefontsize,
        legend=:topleft,
        legendfontsize=legendfontsize,
        left_margin=2Plots.mm,
        right_margin=2Plots.mm,
        top_margin=2Plots.mm,
        bottom_margin=2Plots.mm,
        size=(1600, 1600)
    )

    plot!(
        p,
        x₂[1:i],
        y₂[1:i],
        linewidth=1,
        color=m2col,
        label=false
    )

    # Central body
    scatter!(
        p,
        [0.0],
        [0.0],
        markershape=:circle,
        markersize=marksize,
        color=Mcol,
        markerstrokecolor=Mcol,
        label="M"
    )

    # Body 1
    scatter!(
        p,
        [x₁[i]],
        [y₁[i]],
        markershape=:circle,
        markersize=round(marksize/2),
        color=m1col,
        markerstrokecolor=m1outcol,
        label="m₁"
    )

    # Body 2
    scatter!(
        p,
        [x₂[i]],
        [y₂[i]],
        markershape=:circle,
        markersize=round(marksize/2),
        color=m2col,
        markerstrokecolor=m2outcol,
        label="m₂"
    )
end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Save GIF
#
# saveat = 1/fps
# fps    = fps
#
# Therefore one simulated second corresponds to one second
# of animation playback.
# ------------------------------------------------------------

gif(
    anim,
    "three_body_system.gif",
    fps=fps
)

As you can see, the orbits majorly and seemingly unpredictably change over time. This is pretty typical in the three-body problem: either the orbits are fairly stable and unchanging, or they are unstable and prone to objects being ejected from the system or colliding with one another.

Gravitational \(n\)-body problem

In this section, we will try to generalize our Lagrangian and system of equations from the previous section so that they are applicable to a gravitational system with an arbitrary number of bodies. Let \(M\) denote the static central mass, \(m_i\) the \(i\)th moving mass, \(r_i\) the distance from the central mass to the \(i\)th moving mass, \(\theta_i\) is the angle between the line formed by \(r_i\) and the positive \(x\)-axis, measured counter-clockwise. Which is essentially how we defined things in Figure .

So kinetic energy for this system should be pretty straight forward, namely it should simply be the sum of the kinetic energy of each individual body, in other words it should be:

\[\begin{aligned} T &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \dfrac{m_i(\dot{r}_i^2+r_i^2\dot{\theta}_i^2)}{2}. \end{aligned}\]
Where \(m_i\) is the \(i\)th moving body. As for potential energy, well there will be terms for the interaction between each of the \(n\) bodies. For interactions between the central mass and the moving bodies, these will simply be of the form \(-\dfrac{GMm_i}{r_i}\). As for the interactions between the moving bodies, their potential energy terms will be of the form \(-\dfrac{Gm_i m_j}{r_{ij}}\) where \(r_{ij}\) is the distance between masses \(i\) and \(j\). Hence:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} x_i &= r_i \cos{\theta_i},&\hspace{0.2cm}x_j &= r_j \cos{\theta_j} \\ y_i &= r_i \sin{\theta_i},&\hspace{0.2cm}y_j &= r_j \sin{\theta_j}. \end{alignedat}\]
Let us define the change in \(x\) between masses \(i\) and \(j\) as \(\Delta x_{ij}\) and the corresponding change in \(y\) is \(\Delta y_{ij}\).
\[\begin{aligned} \Delta x_{ij} &= r_j \cos{\theta_j} - r_i \cos{\theta_i} \\ \Delta y_{ij} &= r_j \sin{\theta_j} - r_i \sin{\theta_i}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence their squares would be:
\[\begin{aligned} \Delta x_{ij}^2 &= r_j^2 \cos^2{\theta_j} + r_i^2\cos^2{\theta_i} - 2r_ir_j \cos{\theta_i}\cos{\theta_j} \\ \Delta y_{ij}^2 &= r_j^2 \sin^2{\theta_j} + r_i^2\sin^2{\theta_i} - 2r_ir_j \sin{\theta_i}\sin{\theta_j}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the squared distance between \(m_i\) and \(m_j\) is, by the Pythagorean theorem:
\[\begin{aligned} r_{ij}^2 &= \Delta x_{ij}^2 + \Delta y_{ij}^2 \\ &= r_j^2 \cos^2{\theta_j} + r_i^2\cos^2{\theta_i} - 2r_ir_j \cos{\theta_i}\cos{\theta_j} + r_j^2 \sin^2{\theta_j} + r_i^2\sin^2{\theta_i} - 2r_ir_j \sin{\theta_i}\sin{\theta_j} \\ &= r_i^2 (\cos^2{\theta_i}+\sin^2{\theta_i}) + r_j^2 (\cos^2{\theta_j}+\sin^2{\theta_j}) - 2r_i r_j (\cos{\theta_i}\cos{\theta_j} + \sin{\theta_i}\sin{\theta_j}) \\ &= r_i^2 + r_j^2 - 2r_i r_j \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}. \end{aligned}\]
Square rooting yields:
\[\begin{aligned} r_{ij} &= \sqrt{r_i^2 + r_j^2 - 2r_i r_j \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}}. \end{aligned}\]
As we can see, \(r_{ij} = r_{ji}\) due to cosine being an even function. Hence the potential energy is:
\[\begin{aligned} V &= -\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{GMm_i}{r_i} + \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j}{r_{ij}}\right). \end{aligned}\]
Why the \(j>i\) on the second sum? Well, to ensure we do not count the same terms but with a different order of their indices (e.g. \(\dfrac{Gm_1 m_2}{r_{12}}\) and \(\dfrac{Gm_2 m_1}{r_{21}}\) are equivalent despite the different order of indices) twice. Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} L &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{m_i (\dot{r}_i^2 + r_i^2 \dot{\theta}_i^2)}{2} + \dfrac{GMm_i}{r_i} + \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j}{r_{ij}}\right) \end{aligned}\]
Next we will calculate \(\dfrac{\partial r_{ij}}{\partial q_k}\). For \(q_k=r_k\):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial r_{ij}}{\partial r_k} &= \dfrac{2r_i \delta_{ik} + 2r_j \delta_{jk} - 2(\delta_{ik}r_j + r_i \delta_{jk})\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}}{2r_{ij}} \\ &= \dfrac{r_i \delta_{ik} + r_j \delta_{jk} - (\delta_{ik}r_j + r_i \delta_{jk})\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}}{r_{ij}} \\ &= \dfrac{\delta_{ik}(r_i - r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}) + \delta_{jk}(r_j -r_i \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)})}{r_{ij}}. \end{aligned}\]
As for \(q_k=\theta_k\):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial r_{ij}}{\partial \theta_k} &= \dfrac{2r_i r_j\sin{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}(\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik})}{2r_{ij}} \\ &= \dfrac{r_i r_j\sin{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}(\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik})}{r_{ij}}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the canonical momentum for \(r_k\) is:
\[\begin{aligned} p_{r_k} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{r}_k} \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{m_i (2\dot{r}_i \delta_{ik})}{2}\right) \\ &= m_k \dot{r}_k. \end{aligned}\]
Hence its time derivative is:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_{r_k} &= m_k \ddot{r}_k. \end{aligned}\]
As for the canonical momentum for \(\theta_k\), it is:
\[\begin{aligned} p_{\theta_k} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_k} \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{m_i (2r_i^2 \dot{\theta}_i \delta_{ik})}{2}\right) \\ &= m_k r_k^2 \dot{\theta}_k. \end{aligned}\]
Differentiating with respect to \(t\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_{\theta_k} &= 2m_k r_k \dot{r_k} \dot{\theta}_k + m r_k^2 \ddot{\theta}_k. \end{aligned}\]
As for \(\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_k}\):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial r_k} &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{m_i (2r_i\delta_{ik} \dot{\theta}_i^2)}{2} - \dfrac{GMm_i\delta_{ik}}{r_i^2} - \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j\left(\delta_{ik}(r_i - r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}) + \delta_{jk}(r_j -r_i \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)})\right)}{r_{ij}^3}\right) \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(m_i \delta_{ik} \left[r_i \dot{\theta}_i^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_i^2}\right] - \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j\left(\delta_{ik}(r_i - r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}) + \delta_{jk}(r_j -r_i \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)})\right)}{r_{ij}^3}\right) \\ &= m_k r_k \dot{\theta}_k^2 - \dfrac{GMm_k}{r_k^2} - \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j\left(\delta_{ik}(r_i - r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}) + \delta_{jk}(r_j -r_i \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)})\right)}{r_{ij}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
The \(\delta_{ik}\) imposes that \(i=k\). Since \(j>i\) we get \(j=k+1, k+2, k+3, ..., n-1\). So we are left only with the \(j\) sum for terms multiplied by \(\delta_{ik}\). As for \(\delta_{jk}\), it imposes that \(j=k\) which then requires, given \(j>i\), that \(i=1,2,3,...,k-1\).
\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_j\left[\delta_{ik}(r_i - r_j \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)} )+ \delta_{jk}(r_j -r_i \cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)})\right]}{r_{ij}^3} &= \sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{k}m_j (r_k-r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_k)})}{r_{kj}^3} + \\ &\sum_{i=1}^{k-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i}m_k (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
We can combine these sums into one, as \(r_{ij}=r_{ji}\) and \(\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}=\cos{(\theta_i-\theta_j)}\):
\[\begin{aligned} \sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{k}m_j (r_k-r_j\cos{(\theta_j-\theta_k)})}{r_{kj}^3} + \sum_{i=1}^{k-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i}m_k (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3} &= \sum_{\substack{i=1 \\ i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i}m_k (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence \(\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial r_k}\) becomes:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial r_k} &= m_k r_k \dot{\theta}_k^2 - \dfrac{GMm_k}{r_k^2} - \sum_{\substack{i=1 \\ i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i}m_k (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the \(r_k\) equation of motion is:
\[\begin{aligned} m_k\ddot{r}_k &= m_k r_k \dot{\theta}_k^2 - \dfrac{GMm_k}{r_k^2} - \sum_{\substack{i=1 \\ i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i}m_k (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Dividing by \(m_k\) yields:
\[\begin{align} \ddot{r}_k &= r_k \dot{\theta}_k^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_k^2} - \sum_{\substack{i=1 \\ i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_{i} (r_k-r_i\cos{(\theta_k-\theta_i)})}{r_{ik}^3}. \tag{59} \end{align}\]
As for \(\theta_k\), the canonical momentum and its time derivative are:
\[\begin{aligned} p_{\theta_k} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_k} \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \left(\dfrac{2m_i r_i^2 \dot{\theta}_i \delta_{ik}}{2}\right) \\ &= m_k r_k^2 \dot{\theta}_k. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the time derivative of \(p_{\theta_k}\) is:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_{\theta_k} &= 2m_kr_k \dot{r}_k \dot{\theta}_k + mr_k^2 \ddot{\theta}_k. \end{aligned}\]
As for the other component of Equations (32):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_k} &= -\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_im_j}{r_{ij}^2} \dfrac{\partial r_{ij}}{\partial \theta_k} \\ &= -\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \sum_{j>i}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_im_jr_i r_j\sin{(\theta_j-\theta_i)}(\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik})}{r_{ij}^3} \\ &= \sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\dfrac{Gm_k m_jr_k r_j\sin{(\theta_j-\theta_k)}}{r_{kj}^3} - \sum_{i=1}^{k-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_kr_i r_k\sin{(\theta_k-\theta_i)}}{r_{ik}^3} \\ &= \sum_{\substack{i=1\\ i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_kr_i r_k\sin{(\theta_i-\theta_k)}}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the Euler-Lagrange equations for \(\theta_k\) can be expressed as:
\[\begin{aligned} 2m_kr_k \dot{r}_k \dot{\theta}_k + mr_k^2 \ddot{\theta}_k &= \sum_{\substack{i=1\\i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i m_kr_i r_k\sin{(\theta_i-\theta_k)}}{r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Dividing both sides by \(mr_k^2\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{2\dot{r}_k\dot{\theta}_k}{r_k} + \ddot{\theta}_k &= \sum_{\substack{i=1\\i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i r_i \sin{(\theta_i-\theta_k)}}{r_k r_{ik}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Moving the first term on the left-hand side to the right-hand side yields:
\[\begin{align} \ddot{\theta}_k &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_k\dot{\theta}_k}{r_k} + \sum_{\substack{i=1\\i\neq k}}^{n-1} \dfrac{Gm_i r_i \sin{(\theta_i-\theta_k)}}{r_k r_{ik}^3}. \tag{60} \end{align}\]

Three-body problem comparison

An important verification of our working is that if \(n=3\), we should get the same equations as we obtained in gravitational three-body problem. If \(n=3\), we get for \(\ddot{r}_1\):

\[\begin{aligned} \ddot{r}_1 &= r_1\dot{\theta}_1^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_1^2} - \dfrac{Gm_2(r_1 - r_2\cos{(\theta_1-\theta_2)})}{r_{12}^3} \\ &= r_1\dot{\theta}_1^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_1^2} - \dfrac{Gm_2(r_1 - r_2\cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})}{r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
This matches Equation (50). As for \(\ddot{r}_2\):
\[\begin{aligned} \ddot{r}_2 &= r_2\dot{\theta}_2^2 - \dfrac{GM}{r_2^2} - \dfrac{Gm_1(r_2 - r_1\cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})}{r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Which matches Equation (52). As for \(\ddot{\theta}_1\):
\[\begin{aligned} \ddot{\theta}_1 &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_1\dot{\theta}_1}{r_1} + \dfrac{Gm_2r_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_1 r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Which matches Equation (53). As for \(\ddot{\theta}_2\):
\[\begin{aligned} \ddot{\theta}_2 &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_2\dot{\theta}_2}{r_2} + \dfrac{Gm_1r_1 \sin{(\theta_1-\theta_2)}}{r_2 r_{12}^3} \\ &= -\dfrac{2\dot{r}_2\dot{\theta}_2}{r_2} - \dfrac{Gm_1r_1 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}}{r_2 r_{12}^3}. \end{aligned}\]
Which matches Equation (54). Hence our \(n\)-body problem equations match our three-body problem equations when \(n=3\).

Simulation

Code Listing produces the animation in Figure . It solves Equations (59) and (60) for \(n=4\) with the following initial conditions. We assume each of the orbits start out elliptical and \(a\) is their semi-major axis and \(e\) is their eccentricity. These parts of the initial conditions are:

\[\begin{alignedat}{3} a_1 &= 5,&\hspace{0.2cm} a_2 &= 5,& \hspace{0.2cm} a_3 &= 10 \\ e_1 &= 0.20,&\hspace{0.2cm} e_2 &= 0.05,& \hspace{0.2cm} e_3 &= 0.01. \end{alignedat}\]
We will start each of the moving masses at their periapsis. From this, we get the initial conditions Equations (55), (56), (57), and (58). As for \(G\) and the masses, they are the same as for the three-body problem simulation, except:
\[\begin{aligned} m_3 &= 0.01. \end{aligned}\]
This relatively small \(m_3\) was chosen to represent potentially a planet in this system whereas the other masses are potentially stars. Why did I choose this? Because this system reminds me of the Netflix series 3 Body Problem. As for the initial angles they are the same as for the three-body problem simulation, except:
\[\begin{aligned} \theta_3(t_0) &= \dfrac{\pi}{3} + 0.06. \end{aligned}\]
The lack of most units is deliberate, as we are not analysing a real system. The numbers were chosen just to make the system's behaviour interesting.

N-body problem simulation code. 
using DifferentialEquations
using Plots, Measures, Colors
using Printf, LaTeXStrings
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"

# ============================================================
# N-body gravitational system with a fixed central mass
#
# M is fixed at the origin.
# N orbiting bodies interact gravitationally with M and with
# one another.
#
# State vector:
# u = [r₁,...,rₙ, θ₁,...,θₙ, ṙ₁,...,ṙₙ, θ̇₁,...,θ̇ₙ]
#
# All quantities use normalized units.
# ============================================================

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Parameters
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Add or remove entries to change the number of orbiting bodies.
# All parameter vectors must have the same length.
G              = 1.0
M              = 3.0
masses         = [1.5, 2.0, 0.01]
N              = length(masses)
# Initial conditions
## Semi-major axes
a              = [5.0, 5.0, 15.0]
## Eccentricities
e              = [0.20, 0.05, 0.01]
## Initial angular positions
θ₀             = [
    0.0
    π - 0.15
    π/3 + 0.06
]
# Frames per second of animation
fps            = 60
# Solution params
tspan          = (0.0, 60.0)
abstol         = 1e-11
reltol         = 1e-11
stepsize       = 8/fps
# Animation params
marksize       = 10
legendfontsize = 24
guidefontsize  = 24
tickfontsize   = 20
titlefontsize  = 30

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Validate parameters
# ------------------------------------------------------------
@assert N >= 1 "At least one orbiting body is required."
@assert length(a) == N "The semimajor axis vector must have N entries."
@assert length(e) == N "The eccentricity vector must have N entries."
@assert all(>(0), masses) "All orbiting masses must be positive."
@assert all(>(0), a) "All semimajor axes must be positive."
@assert all(x -> 0 <= x < 1, e) "Eccentricities must satisfy 0 ≤ e < 1."

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial approximate Keplerian orbits
# ------------------------------------------------------------

# Periapsis distances

r₀ = a .* (1 .- e)

# Initial Keplerian periapsis velocities

v₀ = sqrt.(G .* M .* (1 .+ e) ./ r₀)

# Radial velocities vanish at periapsis.

ṙ₀ = zeros(N)

# Since v = r θ̇:

θ̇₀ = v₀ ./ r₀

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Initial state vector
# ------------------------------------------------------------

u₀ = vcat(
    r₀,
    θ₀,
    ṙ₀,
    θ̇₀
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Equations of motion
# ------------------------------------------------------------

function n_body!(du, u, p, t)

    G, M, masses, N = p

    # Obtain views of the state vector.

    r  = @view u[1:N]
    θ  = @view u[N+1:2N]
    ṙ = @view u[2N+1:3N]
    θ̇ = @view u[3N+1:4N]

    # Obtain views of the derivative vector.

    dr  = @view du[1:N]
    dθ  = @view du[N+1:2N]
    dṙ = @view du[2N+1:3N]
    dθ̇ = @view du[3N+1:4N]

    # First-order kinematic equations.

    dr .= ṙ
    dθ .= θ̇

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Gravitational accelerations
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    for k in 1:N

        # Angular differences relative to body k.

        Δθ = θ .- θ[k]

        # Distances between body k and every other body.

        rᵢₖ = sqrt.(
            r[k]^2 .+
            r.^2 .-
            2 .* r[k] .* r .* cos.(Δθ)
        )

        # Exclude self-interaction.

        rᵢₖ[k] = Inf

        # ----------------------------------------------------
        # Radial acceleration
        # ----------------------------------------------------

        radial_interaction = sum(
            masses .* (
                r[k] .- r .* cos.(Δθ)
            ) ./ rᵢₖ.^3
        )

        r̈ₖ =
            r[k]*θ̇[k]^2 -
            G*M/r[k]^2 -
            G*radial_interaction

        # ----------------------------------------------------
        # Angular acceleration
        # ----------------------------------------------------

        angular_interaction = sum(
            masses .* r .* sin.(Δθ) ./ rᵢₖ.^3
        )

        θ̈ₖ =
            -2*ṙ[k]*θ̇[k]/r[k] +
            G*angular_interaction/r[k]

        dṙ[k] = r̈ₖ
        dθ̇[k] = θ̈ₖ

    end
end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Integrate
# ------------------------------------------------------------

p = (G, M, masses, N)

prob = ODEProblem(
    n_body!,
    u₀,
    tspan,
    p
)

solut = solve(
    prob,
    Vern8(),
    saveat=stepsize,
    abstol=abstol,
    reltol=reltol
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Convert polar coordinates to Cartesian coordinates
# ------------------------------------------------------------

x = [
    solut[k, :] .* cos.(solut[N+k, :])
    for k in 1:N
]

y = [
    solut[k, :] .* sin.(solut[N+k, :])
    for k in 1:N
]

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Calculate pairwise separations
# ------------------------------------------------------------

separations = Dict{Tuple{Int,Int},Vector{Float64}}()

for k in 1:N-1
    for i in k+1:N

        separations[(k,i)] = sqrt.(
            (x[k] .- x[i]).^2 .+
            (y[k] .- y[i]).^2
        )

    end
end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Plot limits
# ------------------------------------------------------------

x_all = vcat([0.0], x...)
y_all = vcat([0.0], y...)

xmin = minimum(x_all)
xmax = maximum(x_all)
ymin = minimum(y_all)
ymax = maximum(y_all)

span = max(xmax - xmin, ymax - ymin)

margin = 0.08 * span

xc = (xmin + xmax)/2
yc = (ymin + ymax)/2

xmin = xc - span/2 - margin
xmax = xc + span/2 + margin

ymin = yc - span/2 - margin
ymax = yc + span/2 + margin

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Colours
# ------------------------------------------------------------

palette = [
    RGB(0.0, 0.0, 0.65),   # Dark blue
    RGB(0.0, 0.55, 0.55),  # Dark teal
    RGB(0.65, 0.0, 0.0),   # Dark red
    RGB(0.45, 0.0, 0.65),  # Purple
    RGB(0.0, 0.45, 0.15),  # Dark green
    RGB(0.65, 0.25, 0.0),  # Dark orange
    RGB(0.25, 0.25, 0.25), # Dark grey
    RGB(0.0, 0.4, 0.65)    # Steel blue
]

bodycolors = [palette[mod1(k, length(palette))] for k in 1:N]

centralcolor = RGB24(65/255, 130/255, 65/255)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Animation
# ------------------------------------------------------------

anim = @animate for frame in eachindex(solut.t)

    p = plot(
        xlims=(xmin, xmax),
        ylims=(ymin, ymax),
        aspect_ratio=:equal,
        xlabel=L"x",
        ylabel=L"y",
        title="$(N+1)-body system — t = $(@sprintf("%.2f", solut.t[frame]))",
        guidefontsize=guidefontsize,
        tickfontsize=tickfontsize,
        titlefontsize=titlefontsize,
        legend=:topleft,
        legendfontsize=legendfontsize,
        left_margin=2Plots.mm,
        right_margin=2Plots.mm,
        top_margin=2Plots.mm,
        bottom_margin=2Plots.mm,
        size=(1600, 1600)
    )

    # Plot trajectories and current positions.

    for k in 1:N

        plot!(
            p,
            x[k][1:frame],
            y[k][1:frame],
            linewidth=1,
            color=bodycolors[k],
            label=false
        )

        scatter!(
            p,
            [x[k][frame]],
            [y[k][frame]],
            markershape=:circle,
            markersize=round(marksize/2),
            color=bodycolors[k],
            markerstrokecolor=bodycolors[k],
            label=latexstring("m_{$k}")
        )

    end

    # Central body.

    scatter!(
        p,
        [0.0],
        [0.0],
        markershape=:circle,
        markersize=marksize,
        color=centralcolor,
        markerstrokecolor=centralcolor,
        label="M"
    )

end

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Save GIF
#
# saveat = 1/fps
# fps    = fps
#
# One simulated second corresponds to one second of playback.
# ------------------------------------------------------------

gif(
    anim,
    "n_body_system.gif",
    fps=fps
)

As you can see, the orbits majorly and seemingly unpredictably change over time. This is pretty typical in the \(n\)-body problem: either the orbits are fairly stable and unchanging (like the orbits of the planets in the Solar System), or they are unstable and prone to objects being ejected from the system or colliding with one another.

Double pendulum

I thought it would be instructive for me to show you how to derive the equations of motion for the simplest sort of double pendulum. One where there is no air resistance, no friction, and the rods are both massless. Then the coordinates of the first bob are:
\[\begin{aligned} x_1 &= l_1 \cos{\theta_1} \\ y_1 &= l_1 \sin{\theta_1}. \end{aligned}\]
As for the simple pendulum, \(v_1^2 = l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2\). As for the second bob, they would be:
\[\begin{aligned} x_2 &= x_1 + l_2 \cos{\theta_2} \\ &= l_1 \cos{\theta_1} + l_2\cos{\theta_2} \\ y_2 &= y_1 + l_2 \sin{\theta_2} \\ &= l_1 \sin{\theta_1} + l_2\sin{\theta_2}. \end{aligned}\]
Velocity components are:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{x}_2 &= -l_1 \dot{\theta}_1 \sin{\theta_1} - l_2 \dot{\theta}_2 \sin{\theta_2} \\ \dot{y}_2 &= l_1 \dot{\theta}_1 \cos{\theta_1} + l_2 \dot{\theta}_2 \cos{\theta_2} \\ v_2^2 &= \dot{x}_2^2+\dot{y}_2^2 \\ &= l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \sin^2{\theta_1} + l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_2^2\sin^2{\theta_2} + 2l_1 l_2\dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2\sin{\theta_1}\sin{\theta_2} + l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2\cos^2{\theta_1} + l_2^2\dot{\theta}_2^2\cos^2{\theta_2} + 2l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{\theta_1}\cos{\theta_2} \\ &= l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 + l_2^2\dot{\theta}_2^2 + 2l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the kinetic energy is:
\[\begin{aligned} T &= \dfrac{m_1l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2}{2} + \dfrac{m_2 \left(l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 + l_2^2\dot{\theta}_2^2 + 2l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right)}{2}. \end{aligned}\]
As for the potential energy:
\[\begin{aligned} V &= m_1 gy_1 + m_2 gy_2 \\ &= m_1 gl_1 \sin{\theta_1} + m_2 g(l_1\sin{\theta_1} + l_2\sin{\theta_2}). \end{aligned}\]
Hence the Lagrangian is:
\[\begin{aligned} L &= T -V \\ &= \dfrac{m_1l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2}{2} + \dfrac{m_2 \left(l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 + l_2^2\dot{\theta}_2^2 + 2l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right)}{2} - m_1 gl_1 \sin{\theta_1} - m_2 g(l_1\sin{\theta_1} + l_2\sin{\theta_2}) \\ &= \dfrac{l_1^2 (m_1 + m_2)}{2} \dot{\theta}_1^2 + \dfrac{m_2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_2^2}{2} + m_2 l_1l_2\dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\sin{\theta_1} - m_2 gl_2 \sin{\theta_2}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the canonical momentums are:
\[\begin{aligned} p_{\theta_1} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_1} \\ &= (m_1+m_2) l_1^2 \dot{\theta}_1 + m_2 l_1 l_2\dot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ p_{\theta_2} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}_2} \\ &= m_2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_2 + m_2 l_1 l_2\dot{\theta}_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ \dot{p}_{\theta_1} &= (m_1+m_2) l_1^2 \ddot{\theta}_1 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ \dot{p}_{\theta_2} &= m_2 l_2^2 \ddot{\theta}_2 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}. \end{aligned}\]
As for \(\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_i}\):
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_1} &= m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \theta_2} &= -m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the Euler-Lagrange equations are:
\[\begin{aligned} (m_1+m_2) l_1^2 \ddot{\theta}_1 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} &= m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ m_2 l_2^2 \ddot{\theta}_2 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} &= -m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2}. \end{aligned}\]
Let us get the second derivatives onto the left with everything else on the right-hand side of the equations:
\[\begin{aligned} (m_1+m_2) l_1^2 \ddot{\theta}_1 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} &= m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} + m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ &= m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ m_2 l_2^2 \ddot{\theta}_2 + m_2 l_1 l_2\ddot{\theta}_1 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} &= m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1(\dot{\theta}_2-\dot{\theta}_1)\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} -m_2 l_1 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1\dot{\theta}_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2} \\ &= -m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2}. \end{aligned}\]
Ideally, we want one equation for \(\ddot{\theta}_1\) (and \(\ddot{\theta}_1\) alone) and another \(\ddot{\theta}_2\). So let us put our system into matrix form:
\[\begin{alignat}{2} \begin{bmatrix} (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 & m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & m_2 l_2^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \ddot{\theta_1} \\ \ddot{\theta_2} \end{bmatrix} &= \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2} \end{bmatrix}. \tag{61} \end{alignat}\]
Inverting the square matrix on the left of Equation (61), which we will call \(\mathbf{A}\), we get:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \mathbf{A}^{-1} &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \cdot m_2 l_2^2 - (m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})^2} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_2^2 & -m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{m_1m_2l_1^2 l_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 - m_2^2 l_1^2l_2^2 \cos^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_2^2 & -m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{m_1m_2l_1^2 l_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_2^2 & -m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_2^2 & -m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]
We will call the right-hand side of Equation (61) \(\mathbf{b}\). Hence the solution to this system of equations is:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} \ddot{\boldsymbol{\theta}} &= \mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{b} \\ \begin{bmatrix} \ddot{\theta_1} \\ \ddot{\theta_2} \end{bmatrix} &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_2^2 & -m_2 l_1 l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} & (m_1+m_2)l_1^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)gl_1\cos{\theta_1} \\ -m_2 l_1l_2 \dot{\theta}_1^2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - m_2 gl_2\cos{\theta_2} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} m_2^2 l_1 l_2^3 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} + m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\cos{\theta_2} \\ -m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} + (m_1+m_2)m_2 gl_1^2 l_2\cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\cos{\theta_1} - (m_1+m_2)m_2l_1^3 l_2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} - (m_1+m_2)m_2 gl_1^2 l_2 \cos{\theta_2} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[m_2^2 l_1 l_2^3 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} + m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 (\cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\cos{\theta_2}-\cos{\theta_1}) \\ -m_2 l_1^2 l_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)m_2gl_1^2 l_2\left[\cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\cos{\theta_1} - \cos{\theta_2}\right] \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[m_2^2 l_1 l_2^3 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} + m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 (\cos^2{\theta_2}\cos{\theta_1}+\sin{\theta_2}\sin{\theta_1}\cos{\theta_2}-\cos{\theta_1}) \\ -m_2 l_1^2 l_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)m_2gl_1^2 l_2\left[\cos{\theta_2}\cos^2{\theta_1}+\sin{\theta_1}\sin{\theta_2}\cos{\theta_1} - \cos{\theta_2}\right] \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[m_2^2 l_1 l_2^3 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} + m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 \left[-\cos{\theta_1}\sin^2{\theta_2} + \sin{\theta_1}\sin{\theta_2}\cos{\theta_2}\right] \\ -m_2 l_1^2 l_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)m_2 gl_1^2 l_2\left[-\cos{\theta_2}\sin^2{\theta_1} + \sin{\theta_1}\sin{\theta_2}\cos{\theta_1}\right] \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[m_2^2 l_1 l_2^3 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + m_2^2 l_1^2 l_2^2 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} + m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 \sin{\theta_2}\left[-\cos{\theta_1}\sin{\theta_2} + \sin{\theta_1}\cos{\theta_2}\right] \\ -m_2 l_1^2 l_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)m_2gl_1^2 l_2 \sin{\theta_1}\left[-\cos{\theta_2}\sin{\theta_1} + \sin{\theta_2}\cos{\theta_1}\right] \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})m_2l_1^2 l_2^2} \begin{bmatrix} m_2^2l_1 l_2^2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 m_2 gl_1 l_2^2 \cos{\theta_1} - m_2^2 gl_1 l_2^2 \sin{\theta_2}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ -m_2 l_1^2 l_2\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)m_2gl_1^2 l_2 \sin{\theta_1}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \dfrac{1}{(m_1+m_2\sin^2{(\theta_2-\theta_1)})l_1 l_2} \begin{bmatrix} l_2 \sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \left[l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 + l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\right]- m_1 g l_2\cos{\theta_1} - m_2 g l_2 \sin{\theta_2}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \\ -l_1\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}\left[m_2 l_2 \dot{\theta}_2^2 \cos{(\theta_2-\theta_1)}+(m_1+m_2)l_1 \dot{\theta}_1^2\right] + (m_1+m_2)gl_1 \sin{\theta_1}\sin{(\theta_2-\theta_1)} \end{bmatrix}. \end{alignedat}\]

Hamiltonian mechanics

In Hamiltonian mechanics, we can usually define the Hamiltonian as:

\[\begin{align} H &= T + V. \tag{62} \end{align}\]
Which is the total energy of our system. More generally we define the Hamiltonian as:
\[\begin{align} H &\stackrel{\text{def}}{=} \sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i - L. \tag{63} \end{align}\]
Equation (63) is called the Legendre transformation of the Lagrangian. So when are Equations (62) and (63) not equivalent? The answer is when our potential energy depends, at least in part, on the generalized velocities. Another difference from Lagrangian mechanics is that we define \(H\) in terms of \(p_i\) and \(q_i\) instead of \(q_i\) and \(\dot{q}_i\).

So what is the Legendre transformation? It takes a function, say \(f(x)\), and it uses its tangent lines and their \(y\) intercepts to create a new function, \(g(p)\) that incorporates all the information that \(f(x)\) contained. Here is a great video that explains the Legendre transformation and its derivation.

The equations of motion with no non-conservative forces are:

\[\begin{align} \dot{p}_i &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial q_i} \tag{64} \\ \dot{q}_i &= \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_i}. \tag{65} \end{align}\]

Derivation

We can derive these equations of motion by considering an infinitesimal change to our path and its effect on \(H\), which we will denote as \(dH\):

\[\begin{align} dH &= \sum_{i=1}^n d(p_i\dot{q}_i) - dL \tag{66} \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^n dp_i \dot{q}_i + p_i d\dot{q}_i - dL \tag{67} \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i dp_i + p_i d\dot{q}_i - \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} dq_i - \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i}d\dot{q}_i. \notag \end{align}\]
To go from Equations (66) to (67), we used the product rule of differentiation, in case you were wondering. Next we use the definition of canonical momentum:
\[\begin{aligned} dH &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i dp_i + p_i d\dot{q}_i - \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} dq_i - p_i d\dot{q}_i \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i dp_i - \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} dq_i \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^n - \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} dq_i + \dot{q}_i dp_i. \end{aligned}\]
Next we can use Equations (32) to simplify our \(\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i}\) term. As remember this, according to Equations (32) is equal to \(\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i}\) which can also be written as \(\dot{p}_i\):
\[\begin{align} dH &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i dp_i - \dot{p}_i dq_i. \tag{68} \end{align}\]
Assuming our Hamiltonian does not explicitly depend on \(t\), we can also write \(dH\) as:
\[\begin{align} dH &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial q_i} dq_i + \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_i}dp_i. \tag{69} \end{align}\]
As this is just the change in \(H\) caused directly by the change in \(q_i\) plus the change in \(H\) that is directly caused by the change in \(p_i\). Equating like terms in the right-hand sides of Equations (68) and (69) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_i &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial q_i} \\ \dot{q}_i &= \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_i}. \end{aligned}\]
Which are Equations (64) and (65), which are known as Hamilton's equations. I should mention this is not the only way to derive Hamilton's equations; it is also possible to derive them directly from the stationary action principle, for instance.

Non-conservative forces

To account for non-conservative forces, we merely add the corresponding generalized force terms to the right-hand side of Equation (64). In other words:

\[\begin{align} \dot{p}_i &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial q_i} + Q_i. \tag{70} \end{align}\]
Hamilton's equations creates a new kind of space that we can visualize our solution on that has dimensions \(q_i\) and \(p_i\). This type of space is known as phase space.

Symplectic integration

There is a class of methods for numerically integrating Hamilton's equations specifically; they are called symplectic integration schemes. Unlike the numerical integration schemes we have previously covered, symplectic integration schemes are designed to specifically keep the Hamiltonian unchanged over time. Other numerical integration techniques when applied to Hamilton's equations with small step sizes or low error tolerances will largely leave the Hamiltonian unchanged in the short-term, simply because the differential equation itself keeps the Hamiltonian unchanged. Over the long-term small errors from approximating the solution with these schemes can accumulate to markedly change the Hamiltonian, however.

Examples

Next we will go over some examples to hopefully help to illustrate how the Hamiltonian approach to mechanics works and its implications.

Simple pendulum

The Hamiltonian for the simple pendulum is:

\[\begin{align} H &= T + V \tag{71} \\ &= \dfrac{ml^2 \dot{\theta}^2}{2} + mgl\sin{\theta}. \tag{72} \end{align}\]
We can use Equation (62) to determine our Hamiltonian here because our potential energy, \(V\), is independent of the generalized velocities. Problem is, however, that this is written in terms of \(q_i\) and \(\dot{q}_i\) not \(q_i\) and \(p_i\). First we need to determine what our canonical momentum is. Equations (37) yields:
\[\begin{align} p_{\theta} &= \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}} \notag \\ p_{\theta} &= ml^2\dot{\theta}. \tag{73} \end{align}\]
Rewriting Equation (73) with \(\dot{\theta}\) as its subject:
\[\begin{align} \dot{\theta} &= \dfrac{p_{\theta}}{ml^2}. \tag{74} \end{align}\]
Substituting Equation (77) into Equation (72) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} H &= \dfrac{ml^2}{2} \left( \dfrac{p_{\theta}}{ml^2} \right)^2 + mgl\sin{\theta} \\ &= \dfrac{ml^2}{2} \dfrac{p_{\theta}^2}{m^2l^4} + mgl\sin{\theta}. \end{aligned}\]
Cancelling out common factors in the numerator and denominator of the kinetic energy term yields:
\[\begin{align} H &= \dfrac{p_{\theta}^2}{2ml^2} + mgl\sin{\theta}. \tag{75} \end{align}\]
Hence the equations of motion are:
\[\begin{align} \dot{p}_{\theta} &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial \theta} \notag \\ &= - mgl\cos{\theta} \tag{76} \\ \dot{\theta} &= \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_{\theta}} \notag \\ &= \dfrac{p_{\theta}}{ml^2}. \tag{77} \end{align}\]
As we can see, Hamiltonian mechanics yields a series of first-order ODEs instead of second-order ODEs. Which is important because Lagrangian mechanics and Newtonian mechanics both yield second-order ODE systems. That being said, if you rewrite Equation (77) to make \(p_{\theta}\) its subject and substitute it into Equation (76) and rearrange it to make \(\ddot{\theta}\) the subject, you will obtain Equation (42).

Code Listing is Julia code for solving Equations (76) and (77) with \(-\dfrac{\pi}{2} + 0.1 \leq \theta_0 \leq \dfrac{\pi}{2}-0.1\) and \(-0.1 \leq p_{\theta_0} \leq 0.1\) and producing a phase plot for the solutions. For all the solutions the gravitational acceleration (\(g\)) was assumed to be 9.81\(\mathrm{m}\cdot \mathrm{s}^{-2}\), \(l\) was assumed to be \(1\)m and \(m\) was assumed to be \(1\)kg. Phase plot for the simple pendulum. 

using DifferentialEquations, Plots, LaTeXStrings
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"
g, m, l = 9.81, 1.0, 1.0

function pendulum!(du, u, p, t)
    du[1] = u[2]/(m*l^2)
    du[2] = -m*g*l*cos(u[1])
end

tspan = (0.0, 10.0)
ics = [(θ₀, pθ₀) for θ₀ in range(-π/2+0.1, π/2-0.1, length=9), pθ₀ in range(-0.1, 0.1, length=3)]

plot()
for u₀ in ics
    local sol = solve(ODEProblem(pendulum!, collect(u₀), tspan))
    plot!(sol, idxs=(1, 2), label=false)
end

xlabel!(L"\theta (\mathrm{rad})")
ylabel!(L"p_{\theta}(\mathrm{kg}\cdot\mathrm{m}^2\cdot\mathrm{rad}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-1})")
title!("Simple pendulum phase plot")
savefig("Phase_plot.svg")
Figure shows the phase plot generated by the above Julia code. Each differently-coloured line represents a different value of \(\theta_0\) and \(p_{\theta_0}\). These elliptical lines are called "orbits" in the phase plot. \(H\) is constant for a given orbit and the different orbits correspond to different \(H\) values.

Dampened single pendulum

In Figure , we see the orbits for a given set of initial conditions are closed systems. But this is for a conservative system; for non-conservative systems, where the Hamiltonian is not constant, the orbits are not closed systems and instead spiral towards the origin. Say we add Equation (44) to the right-hand side of Equation (76). This yields:

\[\begin{align} \dot{p}_{\theta} &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial \theta} + Q_{\theta} \notag \\ &= - mgl\cos{\theta} -\dfrac{\rho C_D Al^3|\dot{\theta}|\dot{\theta}}{2} \tag{78} \\ &= - mgl\cos{\theta} -\dfrac{\rho C_D Al^3\Big|\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{ml^2}\Big|\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{ml^2}}{2} \tag{79} \\ &= - mgl\cos{\theta} -\dfrac{\rho C_D A|p_{\theta}|p_{\theta}}{2m^2 l}. \tag{80} \end{align}\]

Phase plot for the dampened pendulum. 

using DifferentialEquations, Plots, LaTeXStrings
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"
g, m, l = 9.81, 1.0, 1.0
rho, Cd, A = 2.0, 0.05, 5.0

function pendulum!(du, u, p, t)
    du[1] = u[2]/(m*l^2)
    du[2] = -m*g*l*cos(u[1]) - rho*Cd*A*abs(u[2])*u[2]/(2*m^2*l)
end

tspan = (0.0, 100.0)
theta0 = pi/2-0.01
ptheta0 = 0

plot()
solut = solve(ODEProblem(pendulum!, [theta0, ptheta0], tspan))
plot!(solut, idxs=(1, 2), label=false)

xlabel!(L"\theta (\mathrm{rad})")
ylabel!(L"p_{\theta}(\mathrm{kg}\cdot\mathrm{m}^2\cdot\mathrm{rad}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-1})")
title!("Dampened pendulum phase plot")
savefig("Phase_plot_dampened.svg")
Figure shows the phase plot generated by the above Julia code. In this phase plot, \(\theta_0=\dfrac{pi}{2}-0.1\) and \(p_{\theta_0}=0\). As you can see, the orbit is unstable and gradually gets closer to the origin. This is due to energy loss from the system. I am only showing one solution in the phase plot because, as the system is non-conservative, the orbits all spiral towards the origin and this would cause a lot of confusing overlap if I included multiple solutions.

Projectile motion

Let us analyse what happens when a projectile is fired into the air. Let \(x\) represent the horizontal direction and \(y\) represent the vertical direction. Our potential energy here would be \(mgy\), hence it is independent of the generalized velocities and we can use the \(H=T+V\) definition of the Hamiltonian.

\[\begin{aligned} H &= \dfrac{m}{2}(\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2) + mgy. \end{aligned}\]
As for our canonical momentum:
\[\begin{alignedat}{2} p_x &= m\dot{x} \hspace{0.2cm}\implies \dot{x} &&= \dfrac{p_x}{m} \\ p_y &= m\dot{y} \hspace{0.2cm}\implies \dot{y} &&= \dfrac{p_y}{m}. \end{alignedat}\]
Hence our Hamiltonian can be rewritten as:
\[\begin{aligned} H &= \dfrac{p_x^2+p_y^2}{2m} + mgy. \end{aligned}\]
As for air resistance it could be approximated by:
\[\begin{aligned} \mathbf{F}_D &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A|v|\textbf{v}}{2} \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\sqrt{\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2}}{2} (\dot{x}, \dot{y}). \end{aligned}\]
As for our covariant basis vectors they are, since we are using Cartesian coordinates:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial \mathbf{r}}{\partial x} &= (1, 0) \\ \dfrac{\partial \mathbf{r}}{\partial y} &= (0, 1). \end{aligned}\]
Hence the generalized non-conservative force in the \(x\) direction is:
\[\begin{aligned} Q_x &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\sqrt{\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2}}{2} (\dot{x}, \dot{y}) \cdot (1,0) \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\dot{x}\sqrt{\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2}}{2} \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\dfrac{p_x}{m}\sqrt{\dfrac{p_x^2}{m^2}+\dfrac{p_y^2}{m^2}}}{2} \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A p_x\sqrt{p_x^2+p_y^2}}{2m^2}. \end{aligned}\]
As for the generalized non-conservative force in the \(y\) direction, it is:
\[\begin{aligned} Q_y &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\sqrt{\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2}}{2} (\dot{x}, \dot{y}) \cdot (0, 1) \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\dot{y}\sqrt{\dot{x}^2+\dot{y}^2}}{2} \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A\dfrac{p_y}{m}\sqrt{\dfrac{p_x^2}{m^2}+\dfrac{p_y^2}{m^2}}}{2} \\ &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A p_y\sqrt{p_x^2+p_y^2}}{2m^2}. \end{aligned}\]
Our equations of motion are:
\[\begin{aligned} \dot{x} &= \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_x} \\ \dot{y} &= \dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_y} \\ \dot{p}_x &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial x} + Q_x \\ \dot{p}_y &= -\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial y} + Q_y. \end{aligned}\]
Hence for projectile motion they become:
\[\begin{align} \dot{x} &= \dfrac{p_x}{m} \tag{81} \\ \dot{y} &= \dfrac{p_y}{m} \tag{82} \\ \dot{p}_x &= -\dfrac{\rho C_D A p_x\sqrt{p_x^2+p_y^2}}{2m^2} \tag{83} \\ \dot{p}_y &= -mg -\dfrac{\rho C_D A p_y\sqrt{p_x^2+p_y^2}}{2m^2}. \tag{84} \end{align}\]

Code Listing is Julia code for implementing an animation based on Equations (81), (82), (83) and (84).

Projectile motion simulation code. 

using DifferentialEquations
using Plots
using LaTeXStrings
ENV["GKSwstype"] = "100"

# Constants
g = 9.81
m = 1.0
ρ = 1.225
C_D = 0.47
A = 0.01
fps = 30

# Equations of motion
function projectile!(du, u, p, t)
    x, y, px, py = u

    v = sqrt(px^2 + py^2)

    du[1] = px / m
    du[2] = py / m
    du[3] = -(ρ * C_D * A * px * v) / (2m^2)
    du[4] = -m*g - (ρ * C_D * A * py * v) / (2m^2)
end

# Ground condition
function ground_condition(u, t, integrator)
    u[2]
end

# Terminate when the projectile crosses y = 0 while descending
function ground_affect!(integrator)
    terminate!(integrator)
end

ground_callback = ContinuousCallback(
    ground_condition,
    nothing,           # Do nothing when crossing y = 0 upwards
    ground_affect!    # Terminate when crossing y = 0 downwards
)

# Initial conditions
x₀ = 0.0
y₀ = 0.0
vx₀ = 30.0
vy₀ = 30.0

u₀ = [x₀, y₀, m*vx₀, m*vy₀]

# Integrate
tspan = (0.0, 10.0)

prob = ODEProblem(projectile!, u₀, tspan)

sol = solve(
    prob,
    callback=ground_callback,
    saveat=1/fps
)

# Animation limits
xmin = minimum(sol[1, :])
xmax = maximum(sol[1, :])
ymax = maximum(sol[2, :]) * 1.1

# Animation
anim = @animate for i in eachindex(sol.t)

    plot(
        sol[1, 1:i],
        sol[2, 1:i],
        xlabel=L"x\;(\mathrm{metres})",
        ylabel=L"y\;(\mathrm{metres})",
        title="Projectile motion",
        label=false,
        aspect_ratio=:equal,
        xlims=(xmin, xmax),
        ylims=(0, ymax)
    )

    scatter!(
        [sol[1, i]],
        [sol[2, i]],
        label=false,
        markersize=6
    )
end

gif(anim, "projectile_motion.gif", fps=fps)
Figure is an animation of projectile motion created using the above Julia code.

Poisson brackets

Poisson brackets provide us an elegant and powerful way to rewrite Hamilton's equations. If we have \(n\) degrees of freedom and two functions \(f\) and \(g\) that depend on \(p_i\) and \(q_i\) (Cline, 2026) :

\[\begin{align} \{f, g\} &\stackrel{\text{def}}{=} \sum_{i=1}^n \left( \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{\partial g}{\partial p_i} - \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial p_i}\dfrac{\partial g}{\partial q_i} \right). \tag{85} \end{align}\]

Poisson brackets also have several interesting properties, such as antisymmetry:

\[\begin{align} \{A, B\} &= -\{B, A\}. \tag{86} \end{align}\]
Consequently:
\[\begin{align} \{A,A\} &= 0. \tag{87} \end{align}\]
Further, assuming each of the coordinates of the phase space are independent, it follows that:
\[\begin{align} \{q_j, p_k\} &= \sum_{i=1}^n \left( \dfrac{\partial q_j}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{\partial p_k}{\partial p_i} - \dfrac{\partial q_j}{\partial p_i} \dfrac{\partial p_k}{\partial q_i} \right) \notag \\ &= \sum_{i=1}^n \delta_{ij}\delta_{ik} \notag \\ &= \delta_{jk}. \tag{88} \\ \{q_j, q_k\} &= \sum_{i=1}^n \left( \dfrac{\partial q_j}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{\partial q_k}{\partial p_i} - \dfrac{\partial q_j}{\partial p_i} \dfrac{\partial q_k}{\partial q_i} \right) \notag \\ &= 0 \tag{89} \\ \{p_j, p_k\} &= \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\dfrac{\partial p_j}{\partial q_i}\dfrac{\partial p_k}{\partial p_i} - \dfrac{\partial p_j}{\partial p_i}\dfrac{\partial p_k}{\partial q_i}\right) \notag \\ &= 0. \tag{90} \end{align}\]
The quality of two functions, say \(f\) and \(g\), having a Poisson bracket that equals zero is phrased as \(f\) and \(g\) commuting under the Poisson bracket.

If a function \(f(q_i, p_i)\) is not explicitly dependent on \(t\) and only depends on \(t\) indirectly through \(q_i\) and \(p_i\), then, via the chain rule:

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{df}{dt} &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial q_i}\dfrac{d q_i}{dt} + \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial p_i}\dfrac{dp_i}{dt}. \tag{91} \end{align}\]
We can use Hamilton's equations to replace \(\dfrac{dq_i}{dt}\) and \(\dfrac{dp_i}{dt}\):
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{df}{dt} &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial q_i}\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial p_i} - \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial p_i}\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial q_i}. \tag{92} \end{align}\]
Interestingly, the right-hand side is equal to the Poisson bracket of \(f\) with \(H\), in other words:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{df}{dt} &= \{f, H\}. \tag{93} \end{align}\]
Hence commuting with the Hamiltonian under the Poisson bracket is a sufficient condition for a quantity that does not explicitly depend on time to have a zero time derivative. A zero time derivative means the quantity is static over time which means it is conserved.

This quality makes it unsurprising that Hamilton's equations can be rewritten as:

\[\begin{align} \dot{q}_k &= \{q_k, H\} \tag{94} \\ \dot{p}_k &= -\{p_k, H\}. \tag{95} \end{align}\]

Noether's theorem

Noether's theorem essentially states that for every continuous symmetry in the Lagrangian, there is a corresponding conservation law. For instance, time translation symmetry of the Lagrangian yields the conservation of energy. To show this, we will assume our Lagrangian does not explicitly depend on \(t\). That is, \(t\) itself does not appear directly in our Lagrangian. The only time dependence \(L\) has on \(t\) is through the dependence of \(q_i\) and \(\dot{q}_i\) on time. If the Lagrangian did explicitly depend on time, we would expect it not to have time translation symmetry. Say we increase time according to the transformation: \(t\rightarrow t+dt\) where \(dt\) is an infinitesimal change in \(t\).

\[\begin{align} L(q_i(t+dt), \dot{q}_i(t+dt)) &= L(q_i(t), \dot{q}_i(t)) + \dfrac{dL}{dt} dt \tag{96} \\ &= L(q_i(t), \dot{q}_i(t)) + \sum_{i=1}^n\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{dq_i}{dt} dt + \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i} \dfrac{d\dot{q}_i}{dt} dt . \tag{97} \end{align}\]
Subtracting \(L(q_i(t), \dot{q}_i(t))\) from both sides of Equation (97) yields:
\[\begin{align} L(q_i(t+dt), \dot{q}_i(t+dt)) - L(q_i(t), \dot{q}_i(t)) &= \sum_{i=1}^n\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{dq_i}{dt} dt + \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i} \dfrac{d\dot{q}_i}{dt} dt. \tag{98} \end{align}\]
We will call the left-hand side of Equation (98) \(dL\), hence Equation (98) becomes:
\[\begin{align} dL &= \sum_{i=1}^n \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial q_i} \dfrac{dq_i}{dt} dt + \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i} \dfrac{d\dot{q}_i}{dt} dt. \tag{99} \end{align}\]
We will substitute Equations (33) into Equation (99), which yields:
\[\begin{align} dL &= \sum_{i=1}^n dt\left(\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i}\dot{q}_i + \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i}\ddot{q}_i\right). \tag{100} \end{align}\]
If you are wondering what about if we have non-conservative forces? Would that not mean Equations (33) would not hold as there would be a missing generalized force term? Yes and in that event the Lagrangian would indeed change in time and conservation of energy would not hold, as our system would be losing energy to those non-conservative forces. Substituting Equations (37) into Equation (100) yields:
\[\begin{align} dL &= dt\sum_{i=1}^n \left(\dfrac{dp_i}{dt} \dot{q}_i + p_i \ddot{q}_i\right) \notag \\ &= dt\sum_{i=1}^n \left(\dot{p}_i \dot{q}_i + p_i \ddot{q}_i\right). \tag{101} \end{align}\]
We can rewrite the bracketed expression on the right-hand side of Equation (101) using the product rule of differentiation as:
\[\begin{aligned} dL &= dt\sum_{i=1}^n \dfrac{d}{dt}\left(p_i \dot{q}_i\right) \\ &= dt\dfrac{d}{dt}\sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i. \end{aligned}\]
If \(L\) does not change in time, \(\Delta L=0\), and hence:
\[\begin{align} dt \dfrac{d}{dt} \sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i &= 0. \tag{102} \end{align}\]
Dividing both sides of Equations (102) by \(dt\) yields:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d}{dt} \sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i &= 0. \end{aligned}\]
\(L\) does not change, so \(\dfrac{dL}{dt}=0\), too. Hence:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d}{dt} \left(\sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i - L\right) &= 0. \tag{103} \end{align}\]
We could now use Equation (63) to simplify Equation (103), but I would like to use this as an opportunity to show you how Equations (62) and (63) are usually equivalent. Interestingly, \(\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i=2T\), that is it is twice the kinetic energy of our system. We can establish this identity using Euler's theorem for homogeneous functions.

Say we have a homogeneous function \(f\) of degree \(m\) of the form:

\[\begin{aligned} f = a_{m+1,1,1,...,1} x_1^m + a_{m,2,1,...,1}x_1^{m-1}x_2 + a_{m-1,2,2,...,1}x_1^{m-2}x_2 x_3 + ... + a_{1,m+1,1,...,1}x_2^m + ... + a_{1, 1, 1, ..., m+1}x_n^m \end{aligned}\]
where \(a_{i,j,k,...,}\) are coefficients that do not depend on our variables. In other words, \(f\) is a polynomial function of \(x_i\) (\(i\in\{1, 2, 3, ..., n\}\)) with a maximum total power of \(m\). Then Euler's theorem states that (Tatum, 2026) :
\[\begin{align} \sum_{i=1}^n x_i \dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_i} &= mf. \tag{104} \end{align}\]
Kinetic energy is a homogeneous function, in terms of \(\dot{q}_i\), of second order, specifically it can be written as:
\[\begin{align} T &= \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij} \dot{q}_i \dot{q}_j. \tag{105} \end{align}\]
Here also \(a_{ij}\) refers to coefficients that do not depend on \(\dot{q}_i\). Hence:
\[\begin{align} \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i \dfrac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{q}_i} &= 2T. \tag{106} \end{align}\]
If we assume that potential energy is independent of \(\dot{q}_i\), then:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i}&=\dfrac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{q}_i}. \tag{107} \end{align}\]
Plugging Equations (107) into Equations (106) gives:
\[\begin{align} \sum_{i=1}^n \dot{q}_i \dfrac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_i} &= 2T. \tag{108} \end{align}\]
Substituting Equations (37) into Equations (108) we get:
\[\begin{align} \sum_{i=1}^n p_i \dot{q}_i &= 2T \tag{109} \end{align}\]

Hence if we substitute Equation (31) into Equation (103), we obtain:

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d}{dt} \left(2T-(T-V)\right) &= 0 \notag \\ \dfrac{d}{dt} \left(T+V\right) &= 0. \tag{110} \end{align}\]
Hence substituting Equation (62) into Equation (110) yields:
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{dH}{dt} &= 0. \tag{111} \end{align}\]
So we see that Equations (62) and (63) are equivalent if and only if potential energy is independent of generalized velocity. We also obtain that the Hamiltonian — which is the total energy of the system if potential energy is independent of generalized velocity — does not change when the Lagrangian is time symmetric, hence it is conserved.

I should also mention that the Hamiltonian formulation of Noether's theorem uses Equation (93). As if a quantity that does not explictly depend on time commutes with the Hamiltonian under the Poisson bracket, this means it does not change over time and hence is conserved.

Hamilton-Jacobi equation

There is a final formulation of classical mechanics that I should probably cover. It is based around the so called Hamilton-Jacobi equation which is of the form:

\[\begin{align} \dfrac{\partial S}{\partial t} + H\left(q_i, \dfrac{\partial S}{\partial q_i}, t\right) &= 0. \tag{112} \end{align}\]
Where \(S\) is the action, which is also called Hamilton's principal function. In this approach, we essentially write our Hamiltonian in terms of \(p_i\) and \(q_i\) and then replace the \(p_i\) with \(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial q_i}\). If our Hamiltonian does not explicitly depend on time, which is usually the case, we then use a separation of variables of the form:
\[\begin{align} S(q_i, t) &= W(q_i)-Et. \tag{113} \end{align}\]
Where \(W\) is called Hamilton's characteristic function or the abbreviated action and \(E\) is the constant energy.

This approach to mechanics actually lead directly to the Schrödinger equation. Schrödinger actually derived his equation from the Hamilton-Jacobi equation. Why? Well, out of the formulations of classical mechanics, the Hamilton-Jacobi equation allows us to describe mechanical systems in a way that is analogous to light waves. The wave-particle duality — the idea that every quantum particle, including electrons and photons, exhibit both wave and particle properties — is a fundamental part of quantum mechanics and hence the Hamilton-Jacobi equation provides a particularly natural starting point for the development of the central equation of quantum mechanics. For an explanation for how we can describe a mechanical system in a way analogous to light waves, I suggest you check out this excellent video by Abide by Reason.

Example: simple pendulum

To see the Hamilton-Jacobi equation in action, let us apply it to the simple pendulum. Replacing \(p_{\theta}\) with \(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial \theta}\):

\[\begin{aligned} H &= \dfrac{\left(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial \theta}\right)^2}{2ml^2}+mgl\sin{\theta}. \end{aligned}\]

Substituting into Equation (112) yields:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\left(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial \theta}\right)^2}{2ml^2}+mgl\sin{\theta} + \dfrac{\partial S}{\partial t} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]

Substituting Equation (113) yields:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\left(\dfrac{dW}{d\theta}\right)^2}{2ml^2}+mgl\sin{\theta} -E &= 0. \end{aligned}\]

Multiplying both sides by \(2ml^2\):

\[\begin{aligned} \left(\dfrac{dW}{d\theta}\right)^2 + 2m^2gl^3\sin{\theta}-2ml^2E &=0. \end{aligned}\]

Rearranging yields:

\[\begin{aligned} \left(\dfrac{dW}{d\theta}\right)^2 &= 2ml^2E - 2m^2gl^3\sin{\theta}. \end{aligned}\]

Square-rooting both sides yields:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{dW}{d\theta} &= \pm\sqrt{2ml^2E-2m^2gl^3\sin{\theta}} \\ &= \pm \sqrt{2ml^2(E-mgl\sin{\theta})}. \end{aligned}\]

Integrating yields:

\[\begin{aligned} W(\theta) &= \pm\sqrt{2m}l \int \sqrt{E-mgl\sin{\theta}} d\theta. \end{aligned}\]

Next, we assume \(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial E}\) will equal a constant, say \(\beta\). Then we differentiate Equation (113):

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\partial W}{\partial E} - t &= \beta. \end{aligned}\]

Rearranging yields:

\[\begin{aligned} t+\beta &= \dfrac{\partial W}{\partial E}. \end{aligned}\]

Hence:

\[\begin{align} t+\beta &= \pm \sqrt{2m}l\int \dfrac{1}{2\sqrt{E-mgl\sin{\theta}}} d\theta \notag \\ &= \pm \int \sqrt{\dfrac{ml^2}{2(E-mgl\sin{\theta})}} d\theta. \tag{114} \end{align}\]
The integrand — given it is integrated with respect to \(\theta\) to obtain \(t\) (plus some constant) — must be \(\dfrac{dt}{d\theta}\) which is one over \(\dfrac{d\theta}{dt}\) or \(\dot{\theta}\). Therefore:

\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{dt}{d\theta} &= \pm \sqrt{\dfrac{ml^2}{2(E-mgl\sin{\theta})}}. \end{aligned}\]

Hence \(\dot{\theta}\) is:

\[\begin{align} \dot{\theta} &= \pm \sqrt{\dfrac{2(E-mgl\sin{\theta})}{ml^2}}. \tag{115} \end{align}\]

Squaring Equation (115) yields:

\[\begin{align} \dot{\theta}^2 &= \dfrac{2(E-mgl\sin{\theta})}{ml^2}. \tag{116} \end{align}\]
Differentiating the left-hand side of Equation (116) with respect to \(\theta\):
\[\begin{align} \dfrac{d\dot{\theta}^2}{d\theta} &= 2\dot{\theta}\dfrac{d\dot{\theta}}{d\theta} \notag \\ &= 2\dfrac{d\dot{\theta}}{d\theta}\dfrac{d\theta}{dt} \notag \\ &= 2\dfrac{d\dot{\theta}}{dt} \notag \\ &= 2\ddot{\theta}. \tag{117} \end{align}\]
Differentiating the right-hand side of Equation (116) and equating to the right-hand side of Equation (117) yields:
\[\begin{align} 2\ddot{\theta} &= -\dfrac{-2mgl\cos{\theta}}{ml^2} \notag \\ &= \dfrac{-2g\cos{\theta}}{l}. \notag \end{align}\]
Dividing both sides by \(2\) yields:
\[\begin{align} \ddot{\theta} &= -\dfrac{g}{l}\cos{\theta}. \notag \end{align}\]
Which we can see matches Equation (42).

Connections

Classical mechanics provides the foundation that other fields of physics build on.

For instance, special relativity provides corrections (often called relativistic corrections) to classical mechanics to account for effects that become pronounced at speeds comparable to \(c\), the speed of light in a vacuum. General relativity accounts for these effects, too, but also accounts for effects most noticeable in strong gravitational fields.

Quantum mechanics instead focuses on what happens on small scales, namely the scale of atoms and subatomic particles. Like classical mechanics, quantum mechanics does not account for effects that become pronounced at speeds comparable to \(c\).

Quantum field theory focuses on quantum fields, such as those pertaining to electromagnetism, the strong nuclear force and the weak nuclear force. It also has the distinction of being relativistic — that is, it accounts for effects that become pronounced at speeds comparable to \(c\).

Special relativity

As previously mentioned, in special relativity we can make relativistic corrections to kinetic energy in our Lagrangian and Hamiltonians. Namely, we can define it as:

\[\begin{aligned} T(v) &= \dfrac{mc^2}{\sqrt{1-\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}}} - mc^2 \\ &= mc^2\left(\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}}}-1\right). \end{aligned}\]

Classical limit of kinetic energy

Using the Maclaurin series, we are going to show that \(T(v)\) is well approximated by \(T=\dfrac{mv^2}{2}\) when \(v << c\). To make things simpler for ourselves, we will consider the Maclaurin series of the function:

\[\begin{aligned} f(x) &= \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x}} \\ &= (1-x)^{-1/2}. \end{aligned}\]

(This is as opposed to directly calculating the Maclaurin series of our \(T(v)\). If we directly applied the Maclaurin series formula to \(T(v)\) the derivatives we would need to calculate would get a bit unruly.) In this case:

\[\begin{aligned} f'(x) &= -\dfrac{1}{2}(1-x)^{-3/2} \cdot -1 \\ &= \dfrac{(1-x)^{-3/2}}{2} \\ f''(x) &= -\dfrac{3(1-x)^{-5/2}}{4} \cdot -1 \\ &= \dfrac{3(1-x)^{-5/2}}{4} \\ f^{(3)}(x) &= -\dfrac{15(1-x)^{-7/2}}{8} \cdot -1 \\ &= \dfrac{15(1-x)^{-7/2}}{8} \end{aligned}\]
Where \(f^{(3)}(x)\) denotes the 3rd derivative of \(f\) with respect to \(x\). Note how for the first derivative the coefficient is just \(\dfrac{1}{2}\), for the second derivative it is \(\dfrac{3}{4}\) and for the third it is \(\dfrac{15}{8}\). Seems to me that the denominator is just \(2^n\) where \(n\) is the order of the derivative and the numerator of the coefficient is \((2n-1) \times (2n-3) \times (2n-5)\times ... \times 1\). Hence:
\[\begin{aligned} \dfrac{d^nf}{dx^n} &= \dfrac{\left(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n 2i-1\right)(1-x)^{-(2n+1)/2}}{2^{n}} \hspace{0.1cm}\forall n\in \mathbb{Z}^{+}. \end{aligned}\]
If you need an explanation of the product notation (\(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^{n} 2i-1\)) we used here, see the Product notation section.

Substituting in \(x=0\) into \(f(x)\) and our derivatives yields:

\[\begin{aligned} f(0) &= 1 \\ f'(0) &= \dfrac{1}{2} \\ \dfrac{d^nf}{dx^n}\Big|_{x=0} &= \dfrac{\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n 2i-1}{2^{n}}. \end{aligned}\]

In this case, \(x=\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}\), and we need to multiply by \(mc^2\) and subtract \(mc^2\) to obtain a Maclaurin series for \(T(v)\) and hence:

\[\begin{aligned} T(v) &= mc^2 \left(1 + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{\left(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n 2i-1\right)\left(\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}\right)^n}{2^{n}n!}-1\right) \\ &= mc^2 \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \dfrac{\left(\displaystyle \prod_{i=1}^n 2i-1\right)\left(\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}\right)^n}{2^{n}n!}. \end{aligned}\]
Hence the first few terms of our Maclaurin series are:
\[\begin{aligned} T(v) &= mc^2 \left(\dfrac{v^2}{2c^2}+\dfrac{3v^4}{8c^4}+\dfrac{15v^6}{48c^6}+\dots\right) \\ &= \dfrac{mv^2}{2} + \dfrac{3mv^4}{8c^2} + \dfrac{15mv^6}{48c^4} + \dots. \end{aligned}\]
So what happens when \(v\) is much smaller than \(c\)? Well, we would expect every term with a non-zero power of \(c\) in the denominator to be negligible in its contribution to \(T\) and this leaves only the first term. Hence we obtain that for \(v << c\):
\[\begin{aligned} T(v) &\approx \dfrac{mv^2}{2}. \end{aligned}\]

General relativity

In general relativity, we can use the Einstein-Hilbert action to derive the Einstein field equations (EFE). The EFE are a series of partial differential equations that can be used to calculate the components of the metric tensor, \(g_{\mu\nu}\). The metric tensor is a type of matrix with sixteen total components that allow us to define proper time in curved spacetime. It has sixteen components because \(\mu\) and \(\nu\) can take on four different values each, corresponding to the four dimensions of spacetime (e.g. in Cartesian coordinates they are \(t\), \(x\), \(y\) and \(z\)), so that gives us \(4\times 4=16\) combinations of possible \(\mu\) and \(\nu\) values. Proper time is analogous to distance in spacetime and is the time that passes locally for an observer.

I should mention, in case you are unfamiliar, that in relativity, the passage of time itself is relative. Objects travelling close to the speed of light or in powerful gravitational fields have time pass substantially slower for them than those at lower velocities and in weaker gravitational fields.

The Einstein-Hilbert action is:

\[\begin{align} S &= \dfrac{c^4}{16\pi G} \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} R \sqrt{\left|\det{g_{\mu\nu}}\right|} d^4 x. \tag{118} \end{align}\]
One major difference you may notice between the classical action and the Einstein-Hilbert action is that the classical action is integrated over time whereas the Einstein-Hilbert action is integrated over spacetime (that is what the \(d^4x\) and \(\mathbb{R}^4\) are about). \(R\) is the Ricci scalar which is calculated based on \(g_{\mu\nu}\) and \(\det{g_{\mu\nu}}\) is the determinant of the metric tensor matrix.

Another application of action in general relativity is that it can be used to calculate the path an object will take in curved spacetime. As the path the object follows will be the path that maximizes its proper time. The action for this calculation is:

\[\begin{align} S &= -mc \int_{\lambda_0}^{\lambda_f} \sqrt{\Big| g_{\mu\nu} \dfrac{dx^{\mu}}{d\lambda} \dfrac{dx^{\nu}}{d\lambda}\Big|}d\lambda. \tag{119} \end{align}\]

Why the \(-mc\) coefficient? Well, the negative sign is to ensure that minimizing \(S\) corresponds to maximizing the proper time. As for the \(mc\), it is to get our units right. See the integral itself is in units of metres. \(m\) is in units of kilograms and \(c\) is in units of metres per second. Consequently our units overall are \(\mathrm{kg} \cdot \mathrm{m}^2 \cdot \mathrm{s}^{-1}\). Action is in units of \(\mathrm{J}\cdot \mathrm{s}\), where \(\mathrm{J}\) denotes Joules, the SI standard units of energy. Joules are, in turn, defined as \(\mathrm{kg}\cdot \mathrm{m}^2 \cdot \mathrm{s}^{-2}\). So \(\mathrm{J}\cdot \mathrm{s}\) is \(\mathrm{kg}\cdot \mathrm{m}^2 \cdot \mathrm{s}^{-1}\), which matches the units of our \(S\). I should also explain that in this integral, \(\lambda\) is an arbitrary parameter. Often we choose it to be proper time. Setting \(\delta S=0\) yields the geodesic equations which are a series of ordinary differential equations that maximize proper time.

Quantum mechanics

We have seen in this article that there are multiple different formulations of classical mechanics. Similarly, there are multiple different formulations of quantum mechanics.

Path-integral formulation

One formulation of quantum mechanics is the path-integral formulation developed by Richard Feynmann. In it, we expect our particle or wave to explore all possible paths and we integrate (or sum) over all those paths in order to compute the probability amplitude of our particle. The exploration of all possible paths is a consequence of the randomness of quantum mechanics. Specifically, we compute the integral:

\[\begin{align} K(\mathbf{x}_f, t_f; \mathbf{x}_0, t_0) &= \int_{\mathbf{x}(t_0)=\mathbf{x}_0}^{\mathbf{x}(t_f)=\mathbf{x}_f} e^{\frac{iS(\mathbf{x})}{\hbar}} \mathcal{D}\mathbf{x}. \tag{120} \end{align}\]

Where \(K\) is the propagator, which specifies the probability amplitude for travelling from \(\mathbf{x}_0\) at time \(t_0\) to \(\mathbf{x}_f\) at time \(t_f\). \(S\) is the action of the particle, \(\hbar\) is the reduced Planck's constant, \(i\) is the imaginary number and \(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{x}\) refers to the fact we are integrating over all possible paths. The probability amplitude is a quantity that, if you take the absolute value of, and square this quantity you get the probability of the particle travelling from \(\mathbf{x}_0\) at time \(t_0\) to \(\mathbf{x}_f\) at time \(t_f\).

One major strength of the path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics is that it provides an explanation for how quantum mechanics gives rise to classical mechanics on larger scales.

So, with the background information covered in Prerequisite mathematics in mind, we now know that \(e^{\frac{iS}{\hbar}}\) is like a 2D unit vector as it points in particular direction in the \(xy\) plane and has unit length. Specifically, it points in the direction \(\dfrac{S}{\hbar}\) radians counterclockwise from the positive \(x\) axis. Each integer multiple of \(2\pi\), which corresponds to a complete revolution around the Cartesian plane, that is present in \(\dfrac{S}{\hbar}\) makes no difference to its overall direction. What matters is what is left after subtracting these integer multiples of \(2\pi\).

Interestingly, when we integrate over all possible paths on classical mechanics scales we find that the \(e^{\frac{iS}{\hbar}}\), for most paths, cancel each other out. To see why, we must first start with the fact that \(\hbar\) is a very tiny number — it is approximately \(1.055\times 10^{-34}\mathrm{J}\cdot \mathrm{s}\). Consequently, \(\dfrac{S}{\hbar}\) is a massive number when we are analysing classical mechanical systems. As typically in these scenarios \(S\) is \(\gt0.01\mathrm{J}\cdot \mathrm{s}\). As a result, even a relatively small difference in \(S\) can lead to a majorly different direction for \(e^{\frac{iS}{\hbar}}\).

This leads to the actions of nearby paths often being in opposite directions and cancelling each other when we calculate the path integral. The one exception is the path corresponding to the stationary action. Remember the equation \(\delta S=0\) that we solve to find the path of our classical mechanical system? It says that a tiny change to the stationary action path should make no change (to first order) to the action and consequently, when we integrate \(e^{\frac{iS}{\hbar}}\) for that path and its neighbours, the stationary action path term is the only one that survives. Consequently, in the classical limit our object only explores the path that extremizes the action, and as we have seen an extremized action leads to the equations of motion in classical mechanics.

Operator formalism

Another connection between what we have covered and quantum mechanics is in the operator formalism of quantum mechanics. In it, we represent the state of our physical system using a function called the wavefunction which is denoted as \(\psi\), which can also thought of as a vector, as it is a complex number. We can calculate the wavefunction using Schrödinger's equation:

\[\begin{align} i\hbar \dfrac{\partial \psi}{\partial t} &= \left(-\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}\Delta + V(\mathbf{x})\right)\psi. \tag{121} \end{align}\]

\(V(\mathbf{x})\) is the potential energy and \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian which is essentially just \(\dfrac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} + \dfrac{\partial^2}{\partial y^2}+\dfrac{\partial^2}{\partial z^2}\). The right-hand side of Equation (121) can also be represented by \(\hat{H}\psi\) where \(\hat{H}\) is the Hamiltonian operator and calculates, from the wavefunction, the total energy of our system. Another important link to classical mechanics that I have already alluded to is that Schrödinger's equation was derived using the Hamilton-Jacobi equation. Specifically, Schrödinger used the Hamilton-Jacobi equation to derive an action that he then extremized and out from that popped his equation.

Quantum field theory

Lagrangians, Hamiltonians and actions also appear in quantum field theory, including The Standard Model of particle physics. In fact, if you want to see a dauntingly long and complex Lagrangian, Google The Standard Model Lagrangian.
